LANCASIIIRE COUNFY COUNCiL_PRINCIPAL OF AGRICULTURE. The Lancashire County Council requ.re the sorvices Of a gentleman whose scientific and trCltCOl experience qualities him to lecture on agricuitural science; to carry out agricidtural experiment 3 ; and generally to superin- tend agricultural education throughout tho County. The salary will be £550 per annum, and the County Council will in addition pay all proper travelling ex- penses and other necessary disbursements for the due performance ef the duties of the office. The Principal of Agriculture will be required to devote the whole of his time to the duties of his gffice, and not to undertake env other employment, and will be sub- ordinate to the Director of E location for the County. The engagement to be terminath by three months' notice on either side.

Applications accompanied by not more than four testimonials must be ssrit addressed to The Clerk of the County Council," County Offices, Preston, on or before the 28th day of April. and marked " Principal of Agriculture."

HARCOURT E. CLARE, Clerk of the Coittty Council. Ceutity Offices, Preston, 2nd April. 1902

ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

Eastertide as usual brought with it a joy* Cusness which took visible expression in a multitude of gay hats and dainty toilettes. There is annually at this time a rush of novelties in the world of dress, and this season affairs sartorial seem to have made a greater advance t' n usual. It seems, now-a-days, that people, for the most part, have made up their minds to do without any denii-saison toilettes and substitute instead the more sensible plan of making their winter outfit serve till now by the kindly aid of a few renovating touches. We seem this season to have thrown off our winter chrysalis to emerge in all the glory of a summer butterfly, regardless of the fact that the sunshine is intermittent and that the air is still cold. I fancy the fact of last year's Ilepressing period of national mourning has much to do with this rebound, and that many, in the relief at being able to allow their natural taste for colours full play, are apt to go to almost ridiculous extremes. Be that as it may, there is a charm in dress as now evinced -which is certainly irresistible, and it requires great strength of mind to withstand the allure- ments displayed on all sides for our undoing. One cannot but confess that even the choice of -a hat or toque presents great difficulties, as, however firmly one's mind is made up as to -what we think the most suitable kind of headgear for our requirements, when it comes to actually making a selection among the many .charming novelties we ve apt to waver weakly, and in the end forsake our first love for some-

thing still more fascinating. My sketch, how- ever, is not devoted to any of the elaborate millinery triumphs, lint to a style of hat which will this season make its influence felt and eez4ously imperil the popularity of the ever useful. sailor hat. This hat, to be a success, must cost half-a-guinea, as the cheaper imita- tions. of the real Panama are hard and never look the same as the good qualities of the proper article, and are descernable at a glance. For both spring and early summer wear this mode will be much in vogue, and later on in the season it will make its presence notable at seaside resorts and up the river. One of its great advantages is, that when a good sample of this hat has to be packed it can be rolled up in the same manner as those favoured by our masculine relatives and friends, and it there- fore takes up particularly little space when one is paying a succession of visits. It always resumes its normal shape on being released. By the bye, I wonder if it has occurred to my readcn that the sailor hat has this season

, developed a frivolous tendency to a turned up brim tilted over the face by means of a black velvet cache-peigne at the back. We hardly recognise our old friend in this guise, and consqle ourselves with the reflection • that the sensible sailor hat is still much en evidence.

-1,1 •

The smart little coat illustrated is an excellent sample of one of the most useful and fashion- able coats at present, which, while suitable for

A IIIIONABLE COAT. Immediate wear, 77P#1 also be considered' aid all summer. It is m 444 of black &cc; silk much -tucked and stitchedith a butterfly basque at the back, and a dainiy• collar appliqued with lace. Empire sac "coats of varying lengths exprested in glace or taffetas silks are much in voeue, and come to fill the va6ancy left by the fur coats we, are perforce obliged to abandon during theYearlier part of the day at all events.,

The skirts of our gown. Madame la Mode has decided, must no longer flop round our feet in clinging folds, the fulness. which is as great as ever. being arranged instead to stand out all round the hem in flute-like folds. To get this effect very skilful cutting is necessary, and in some instances-an interlining of muslin is used to keep the folds chsp. The most fashionable people have their skirts so inter- lined as far as thV knees, where the ordinary lining of glace silk also ceases, the remainder of the skirt funny the knees upwards being lined alone with soft China silk or batiste. This produces the clinging effect which is correct at the upper part of the figure, and gives an admirable outstanding appearance at the hem. The smart toilette illustrated was treated in this fashion„ and is expressed in pale green .cloth trimmed with rich lace, and a touch of pleasant contrast is given by some black satin ribbon which forms ;a chow and ends. The iwell-cut plain skirt, as up-to-date, is eminently

THE EXAMINER FRII)AY APRIL ll 1902

[ALL RIGHTS RzsEETED.]

LADY CLARA.'S CANVASS,

FREDERIC ADYE (Author of "The Queen of the Moor," ie.)

PART 1.-CHAP TEII I.

The whole country had gone wild over the General Election. The late Government had died a natural death, and the duty devolved upon the nation of choosing its successors. The recent re-distribu- tion of seats and extended franchise supplied a new interest to the time-honoured occasion, and the edi- fying spectacle of a free and enlightened electorate choosing, with the acumen Ind independence which have eyer been its admired characteristics, the fittest persons to represent its manifold interests in Parlia- ment, was being once more presented, this time with a fresh cast of actors and on an enlarged stage. The excitement, though as a matter of course it seethed most fiercely in the larger towns where the tide of party feeling ran always high, and higher now than ever, was not conlined to populous places, but extended to the remotest country districts as well. The district with which we have to do was styled on electoral maps, Dampshire S.W., and in south-western Dampshire the election fever was very prevalentiindeed. As was to be expected the newly- enfranchised and their families proved the most sus- ceptible subjects. Neverthelessit attacked all classes, and others to whom the possession of a vote was not a new delight, succumbed to the epidemic. As the Election drew near there was great canvassing throughout Dampshire. Men of business neglected thtir own affairs to further the interests of their chosen candidate. Men of leisure, to whom a long desuetude had rendered work of any kind distasteful, met and talked for their cause in the market-places of the little towns or in the smoking-room of the County Club. And the ladies—how they worked! After the crushing defeat which they had experienced at the last General Election the Conservative leaders had betaken themselves to organisation a prominent result being the formation of the well-known Prim- rose League, the dames of which worked with an energy which confounded such of the enemy as chanced to be ignorant of the capacity of the sex when roused to action.

The keenest of all these doughty dames in south- west Dampshire was undoubtedly the Lady Clara Chaceley, younger daughter of the Earl of Forth- ampton. As nice a girl as you would find in a day's side was Lady Clara; twenty-six at this period, and fancy-free. Her elder sister, who was by way of being a beauty, had, long before reaching Lady Clara's age, paired herself off in brilliant style with the heir-apparent to a marquisate. Yet many who knew them both maintained that Lady Clara was the nicer of the two' and all the world wondered that no eligible suitor had come a-wooing so fair and gracious a maid. You never saw anything like the way in which Lady Clara worked over this election. She seemed to be imbued with the very spirit of faction, and fought against the Liberal candidate tooth and nail— I use this expression advisedly, for it veritably seemed as though she might have bitten and scratched him, had that obnoxious person ventured to come near ter during the canvass.

Her energy was the more surprising in that she had never before betrayed more than the most languid interest in polities. Elections had taken place, members been returned or rejected, without ruffling a feather of this golden pheasant's plumage; now she was fierce as a hawk whenever the subject of politics came up. Why was it thus with her? —True, the Conservative candidate, the Hon. Temple Goring, Lord Pangbourne's son, was her cousin; but her affection for him was of the mildest charm- tar ; there could not be a soupcon of romance in it, for Temple Goring was a married .man of middle age, with an amiable wife and a nursery full of chil- dren. Nor was Lady Clara achampioness of woman's rights (in which case, I imagine, she would have been upon the other side). She desired no vote of her own; but she did desire the votes of other people, and she moved heaven and earth to get them. The mainspring of her activity was the sincere convic- tion that she must work, and that everybody must work who could be made to work, to save the Con- stitution, to save the Establishment, to save her own order k What the country would do without her order Lady Clara could not conceive. Chaos must ensue. A Republic 2—A Republic was chaos. How could society go on amid confusion of ranks, and without definitive tables of preceden9e ? How would foundation stones get themselves laid ? statues be unveiled ?—exhibitions opened ?—fancy- fairs held ?—if Lady Clara's order should cease to -ist ? She had thought it all out, and in this crisis affiiirErbad recognised the obligation—other own personal canvass. Canvass she did, with all her soul and strength, morn, noon and -eve, in sunshine and rain, up hill and down dale, through hamlet and village, field and farm. At all hours was to be seen her pretty phaeton whirling along,lhe country lanes, her ponies trotting so swiftly that their slender legs could scarce be seen, or the spokes of her humming wheels. Now she drew rein at cottage door, whereat alighting she would pass within to pet the white- headed chubby-checked children, nod coax with win- ning ways the labourer's wife. Further on she would pull up by the five-barred gate to accost the new voter himself at work in the • fields. Then would John Plowman stay his team to gaze open-mouthed at the unwonted apparition of a real lady picking her way towards him over ridge and furrow, gruciouS as Ceres herself come to bless his honest toil.

exist? She ot affrurs bad

Vote for her cousin, Mr. Goring, would he not, and do her, Lady Clara, such a favour a., she should never forget? John might have made up his mind beforehan to vote for " Vother genieman;" but then to hear the irresistible way in which she Would cut up the Liberal programme, and •persuade him that true blue was the only wear ! Who could resist such charming manners, such a gracious presence? John wavers, hesitates, stutters, finally promises. She enters ,his name with a golden pencil on the ivory tablets depending from her slender waist, places her dainty fingers for a moment in his horny and re- luctant fist, and with a smile of .sweetness is gone to regain her carriage ; leaving .-lohn plafte. in. mid- furrow, flattered yet puzzled, rubbing :1, bewildered pate, within which already dawns comb dim con- sciousness that he has been unfairly dealt -With, and should not have been made ttePreirtise his precious brand-new vote in this-off-hand way. Dazed with his enchanting vision he resumes his toil; to-morrow haply he will repudiate the premise altogether. But it was not only in country lanes that Lady Clara carried on her canvass. Into the towns she went as well, penetrating with a conselentleas dar- ing its most squalid courts and alleys in pursuit of her prey. Lady Clara's town wa.sDanchtister on the Dank, a busy place of moderate dime'ustons, yet with a teeming population. In the old days, had Lady Clara gone a-canvassing she cculd have bought votes by the-soore. Now he could only work for them, which she did most systematically, diving down into the most unsavoury purlieus of the river -side with a zeal not to be surpassed. • .Yet there was, nothing ontrA or forward in her *thods.'• She did ' not attempt to. buy votes with kisses, as did that bold duchess in the days of Fox; nor did she harangue with feminine shrillness her brickeys and bargees; but justly strove with all lady-like delicacy, and the dainty suavity of refined womanhood to h:ttil. these sons of toll into the better '.political way. , Some- times she was well received, sometimes not. At best her success was dubious; for the riverside was a hot bed f of Radicalism, in which a Conservative principle was as scarce as a clean shirt. Still she persevered with a sell-devotion which amounted to heroism. As a Alla the quiet grace of her ladyhood compelled respect, even from those who would have none. of her . polities. Occasionally, however, she met with a stinging rebuff, most often from her own sex, as when a red-faced, shrill-tongued dame, whom she canvassed of the wash-tub, tilt her steaming arms a-kimbo On her broad hips, and thus spoke-- Myleddy us have a'livecl here - going on for twenty year, and never afore to-day has us ever seen your face down. our court. We plight ha' been clemmed hunger and famished.. wr. cold in the shard times afore you, or the likes o' you'd found of us out; but now. so ,Vorsa's we got sum- - mat o' our own as you -wants, yiu comes to get it from us to once !" Unjust as this accusation really was (for Lady Clara was charity personified in her visitation of the sick and poor in her own parish; • albeit nothing Thad. . ever brought her before into the slums of Dandles- ter) yet it was so indisputably true in fact thth she could but blush with hot shame and retire, which she did -with both -ears tingling, as if the robust washer-woman had soundly boxed them. But one raw afternoon in November, a few days before the nomination was to take place, Lady Clara net with a little adventure in the streets of Dan- ,nester. She had finished her last campaign in the slums of that damp and dirty town; had tucked her- ;€o.,i comfortably up in the warm furs of her waiting carriage, and the impatient ponies were stampeding homewards, /their quick hoofs Reuttering on the greasy fla,gs in the narrow lanes of the lower quarter of the town, when suddenly there was a sharp scream as hasty feet.tylipiullti-, and a child down under the ponies'', Thereupon a considerable tumult arose. • Mothers ran out-of-doors vociferating, each one to see if the brat were_dier own: and tindinir it was.net. 71:qtt:P.

more ado thafi ever. rfgrstWFsleifireTfarETl rough; lads and men, excited by the election and ripe for mischief. There had been already some sport that afternoon. But twenty minutes before the Curate had gone up Bridge Street at the best pace he had shown since winning hiscollegequarter-mile in fifty- eight and three-fifths of a second seven years before, outstripping his pursuers indeed, but leaving a large fragment of coat-tail as a trophy in their grimy hands. Dr. Parkyns, a leading Conservative, had narrowly missed colliding with a merry brick-bat which was flying in the market-square as he drove through; while the finest quarry of all, the Con- servative candidate himself, had been hunted almost into the Dank, and was at this moment lying hid in the sanctuary of a wash-house, afforded by a com- passionate female who had witnessed this exciting episode in his canvass. A number of these roughs now crowded round Lady Clara's carriage, blocking the way, and form- ing with the clamouring women quite a small mob. When the child was got out from under the ponies' feet he was insensible, and seemed to be badly in- jured. ,The—nproar increased, some of the women becoming violent, and kindling the fury of the mob with wild accusations against the aristocrat who had recklessly killed a child of the people. In vain Lady Clara apologised and protested, entreating them to lay the child beside her in the carriage that she might drive at once to the hospital and have him. tended. The women waxed furious as they looked upon the little one's pallid face and blood-bedabbled hair; the mob caught the contagion; stones and mud began to fly; the ponies backed and reared; the little groom was knocked down • his mistress. herself in imminent peril. The situation was be- coming critical. Poor Lady Clara! The stones and mud were terrible to her; still more so the oaths of the men, and the fierce faces of the women. Yet she sat erect; reins grasped in quivering hands; colour gone from cheeks blanched with fear; striving with the courage of her race at least not to show her alarm. , Cries were raised at last to pull her out of her carriage and it was doubtful how it would go with her when a knot of well-dressed men appeared on the scene, coming out of an adjacent court. They were but five, and two of them stout and elderly; but seeing how things were they threw themselves at once into the crowd, headed by a fair-haired broad-shouldered man of thirty-fiveor so, who threw away his umbrella and began to hit out right and left from the shoulder with considerable force and science. The crowd gave way before this sudden and unexpected onslaught, and space was quickly cleared for a few yards in front of the carriage.

"Now come on-," he shouted. "The child, I must take the Lady Clara exclaimed.

"All right !"—and. snatching the little boy from the arms of a bystander he sprang with him in be- side Lady Clara, who promptly whipped 'up her ponies and drove off at a gallop, hurling two or three of her assailants to the ground in the sudden vehem- ence of her start. With such vigour did Lady &ara ply her ivory- handled whip that in a very few seconds she found the spirited ponies were away with her. Snorting with indignation at such unwonted treatment they dashed up the lane, striking fire from the kerb as they turned the corner and flew at racing speed through the town, leaving the hooting yelling crowd far behind. Lady Clara was a clever whip, but how she managed to steer them through the narrow streets without a collision she never knew. The people stared, as well they might, at the strange equipage tearing by: a young lady driving like 3 elm the son of Nimshi, furiously, with a hatless man clasping a wounded child in'his arms, and blood trickling from deepish cut on-his own forehead, was a startling spectacle for a quiet provincial town even at election time.

"I am afraid they are out of my hand," said Lady Clara. "They will run home for a certainty, and I want to4stop at the hospital in South Street. What shall we do? My wrists are aching awfully ; they'll never bold out." " Give me the reins, anditake the child," said her unknown companion. "I am not much of a whip, but I'm pretty strong." He was.. He took the reins from her trembling hands, numbed with the strain, into his muscular grasp; and before they had gone fifty yards fur- ther the ponies c)ime to hand, and bent their proud necks in token of submission. The next minute they stood champing their bits before the county hos- pital.

CalprEn 11.

Five days later the wintry sun shone in with pale gleam upon a little bed in the children's ward, in- vesting the small, white .robed sufferer with a glory, and glinting on the fair head of a man who bent over tho—eouoh, endeavouring to amuse its occupant. Toys, picture-books, and comfits, strewed lavishly on the coverlet, showed that the visitor had not come empty-handed. The injured little one not so hurt but tnat he could sit up and play with his treasures, supported by a strong arm beneath his pillow, was evidently disposed to make the best of things, and enjoy life still: though now and then the anguish of the setting bones would be too much for him, and with a moan of pain the little head would go down upon the stalwart shoulder offered for its support, and lie there wearily, yet not without content in so secure a resting place. At such a moment Lady Clara-came ; but stood motionless in the doorway as one stands before a striking picture. She marked the way in which the strong man bent over his charge, soothing with caress tender as a woman's the passing anguish Of the babe, for thel child she had driven over was little more—noted too the gifts he had brought, and the contrast between the gentle touch and the vigour- ous blows he had delivered in her defence. Little given to sentiraentalitA there was something in the scene that touched Lady Clara, and a film- gathered in her eyes. The Man was so abSorbecl with the child that he had not noticed her approach, so Lady Clara drew back within the shelter of a screen, and inquired of the nurse who attended her—

"Who is he ? Is he a 'doctor, or someone living in the town?"

The nurse did not know. The child had been brought in on Saturday with a simple fraeture of tho tibia, and the gentleman had been daily to see him, which was all she knew of the ease. _ _

Announced by the nurse as "a lady," twhich by the way was a self-evident' fact, Lady Clara ad- vanced into the ward and addressed the strangsr,-7.

"I see you are doing your best to repair the .I.amage done by My careless driving the other day; that is kind of you." The stranger bowed, and Lady Clara proceeded— "It was kind of you too to help me in the way you did. lam sorry you had to be so—so energetic. I fear you may have hurt some of the crowd—or ryourself, ' she added as an after thought. ,The stranger smiled, regarding pensively the kiauckles of his left hand, which appeared to have recently suffered abrasion. " I fear I was rough," he admitted, " yet feel sure your ladyship will be disposed to pardon my-- energy, on the reflection that the victims of it were only Radicals." • Lady Clara opened her eyes wide wire amaze- ment—then flushed with anger. Who was this quiet-mannered, well-dressed man who, though an entire stranger, was able to pierce her in her weak- est place? That he should know her was, of course, not surprising; everybody knew Lord Forthamp- tou*s daughter ; but who could he be who thus dared •.,a satirize her political intolerance, of which she had ceen herself unconscious, but which now as by an electric flash was laid bare to her!

Without appearing to notice the discomfiture his mard hitting had caused her, the gentleman now laid ,he child softly down and prepared to depart; but a, paroxysm of pain again seizing the little one, who' clung to him while it lasted, ho was in a manner compelled to remain. Lady Clara watched him with unconscious jeal- ousy. How tender he was to the child, how hard to herself; with so grave a smile and so kindly a light in his clear grey eyes he looked incapable of severity. yet never in her life had Lady Clara been made t wince as ttiis man had made her.

His 'gentleness with the child, however, somewhat disarmed her anger; and presently she found ilex.. self seatecton the opposite sidO of the littla_bed en- gaged in converse with /he stranger. How—she asked—came/he; to he in that part of the town when he had so opportunely come to her assistance ? '

"I was engagea with some friends in canvassing; and. you —were you visiting the poor people?" _ . _ .„

"Yes, with a„ view of itidtteilig them tio'vote pro- perly at the election," said Lady Clara didactically —" -which May also,^l suppose; be described as can- -crossing f" " Quite so—on the other side. The men with me were members of the Liberal Committee !" " Oh," remarked Lady Clara, a little shocked to find herself in such dangerous proximity to a live Radical, only a narrow be and a small child divid- ing hen from the monster. There was something almost fascinating in the situation. She could scarcely remember haying been ad near one before ; her Own set were so exclusively Conservative. Re- garding him furtively she was surprised to observe no visible marks of the beast about him. Indeed he seemed to be a cultivated and agreeable gentleman, and nergonallY mar,. inthrestimx than many of her

brfrillox friends—than I'Mpre'llloilirg, for ffis&nce, who like the Duke in Patience was about as ordinary a young man as you would find, and carried no patent of nobility upon his person, although /Is son of a per. Lady Clara did not go. ilt behoral her. now to stay, and avail herself of this opportunity to say a word in season, and convert if possible this mis- guided youth to better ways. Did he know Mr. Lemarchant, the Radical candi- date, personally ?—she proceeded to ask. The stranger regarded her steadfastly for a mo- ment before he answered. "Yes. I knew something of him at Oxford, and elsewhere. But I should hardly term him a Radical, if you use the term, as I imagine you do, to imply a revolutionist. I can as- sure you he is a sincere upholder of authority; and should he be returned, intends to support the pres- ent constitutional form of government; at any rate until the country is fit for a Platonic Republic, which is not likely to be the case in his day. I should be rather disposed to describe him as a moderate Liber- al, if it be necessary to label him at all." Lady Clara did not know what a Platonic Re- public might be. She had heard of Platonic attach- ments, and did not approve of them, so answered sententiously: _

"All repUblics are dangerous. Mr. Lemarchant is supposed to be a clever man, is he not? Should you consider him so ?" "As men go, I suppose he is." "And is fully aware of it ?" Lady Clara rejoined with a little acerbity. "Doubtless. You may recall a remark of Bulwet Lytton's to. thi effect that no one ever possesses superior intelledthal powers without knowing them. However pretty the alliteration of modesty mid merit may be, he justly observes that the veil of the modesty we admire never disguises its extent from the possessor. My friend is naturally the most humble minded of men; but lately I have heard Lim remark that since he• had come to know more of mankind he feared he was above the average in in- telligence. So far from pluming himself on this dis- covery he seemed rather to regret that the average should not be higher." "I think your friend must be considerably above the average in personal conceit, and can only hope 'that his merits are at least not on a par with his modesty," Lady Clara retorted. "However, I trust he will not have the opportunity of exhibiting his talents in Parliament,ff she added somewhat spite- fully. I fancy he will about win," remarked the stranger quietly. "Then all I can say is," Lady Clara exclaimed hotly, "that he oughtn't to. You must pardon me if I say that I consider it very questionable taste in- deed on his part to come down here, where he is en- tirely unknown, to oppose Mr. Goring", who has re- presented the county for—for--since the last elec- tion," she concluded rather tamely. Now the stranger was unaware of the relationship between his fair interlocutor and the Conservative candidate, wherefore he replied: "That might possibly be a reason why the county should now have a change. But are you sure that Mr. Goring has really represented the county? or granting that he has represented what has hitherto been the majority of voters in it, does it follow that he would equally represent the reformed constitu- ency? Should my friend appear to represent more fully the views of the new voters, why have not they a right to elect him ? He is contesting the seat with perfect fairness."..

" Mr. Goring," said Lady Clara with some hauteur,' yet a little inconsequently, "is my cousin." "Then Mr. Goring is most happy in that rela- tionship," the stranger said politely,:" but you will excuse my pointing out that it hardly confers upon him the exclusive right to represent your county in Parliament."

"The chief part of the land in this division be- longs to my father, who approves of his nephew's candidatur9." "Quite so; but the voters belong to nobody; they are supposed to find someone who shall repre- sent their interests in Parliament, not who shall re- present Lord Forthampton's. He indeed being in the House of Lords represents himself; and more- over being there is precluded by Parliamentary eti- quette from concerning himself with an election to the House of Commons."

This, however, was purely theoretical on the stranger's part, for as a matter of fact Lord Forth- ampton did concern himself very much with the election, and although his dignity, combined with gout, prevented his taking any active measures, ydt he encouraged his daughter's canvass, and was even prepared to drop heavily upon any dependant who should be discovered voting against his nephew. It began now to dawn upon Lady Clara that she would hardly convert her argumentative opponent at one sitting ; so dropping the subject of poli- tics she directed the conversation to other and various topics, in all of which, it seemed to her, he ezhibited the soundest knowledge and best of taste. So inter- esting did they find their converse that it was not until untdusk was gathering in the ward, and the little patient had fallen asleep on his arm, that the strange laid him gently down and prepared to depart. Lady Clara went also, and having allowed him to take her to her carriage and place her in it, actually gave him her hand—a nice, firm, daintily-gloved hand it was—, though a few days before it would have seemed im- possible that she should ever confer such a distinc- tion upon a Liberal canvasser. "Far too nice for a horrid Radical," was Lady Clara's reflection as she drove homewards—" I wish I had discovered his name. How impudent of him to argue with me like that !"

To be Concluded.)

VISIT OF ROYALTY RECALLED. The King and Lord Gerard.

An Incident Now Crops Up.

A. story of the somewhat remarkable experiences of a lady at Ashton-in-Makerfleld, not far from Wigan, which has just come to light, recalls, in a rather peculiar way, the visit some time ago by the King—then the Prince of Wales—to the district. The Heir-Apparent was for a time the guest of Lord Gerard. and the visit will long be remembered by Mr., Mary Jackson, the lady referred to, hying at 73, Gerard-street, Ashton-in-Mekerfield, though at the time when everyone was taking part in the festivities of the Royal visit she was confined to her home. Nevertheless she was able to give a Warrington reporter, who called on her the other day, some interest. leg facts. " I bad been ill for years at that time." sloe said, " and I can recall the Royal visit vividly, for I was at my worst about that time. How- ever I was fortunately cured by Dr Widiams' pink pills for pale people. I had become very low through great weakness, extending over a long period,and I had grave misgiving as to my ultimate recovery. I began to feel the weaknese ccming on years ago, and although I con- sulted many doctors, any improvement that I experi- enced was not long maintained, and I always felt a return of the languid, listless condition. I never seemed equal to my housework, and though I dragged through it, very often I was far from ',Allele fit for the exertion. My appetite was poor, and my ill-health wae quite apparent in my appearance. My illness jut seemed to be a severe case of eeneral debilte. " Fresh sir was the most common recommendation of the doctor., mad, acting ortteeir advice, I frequently, but with difficulty, walked as far As the public park. One occasion I had good cause to remember. One dee, last July, while I was taking a walk, an invalid in a bath chair, noticing how pale I looked, spoke to me and inquired the nature of my Rimers A long conversation ensued, end finally the lady recommended me to take Dr. Williams' pink pills for pfle people. I did so. There was a great improvement after the first box, and my health is now better than I ever expected it would be. I found that after I commenced to take Dr. Williams' pink pills I wan gathering strength daily, my appetite improved and my food seemed to nourish me more. I gradually lost my tired and depressed feeling, and found that:l could get upstaire,wit bout having to rest Several times as I formerly did. Ido all my own house- work now, and I can get through a hard day without the terribly tired and depressed feeling which I formerly experienced. I can also walk about the town without fatigue, whereas before I could not walk ten yards without a rest. I used to be ashamed of myself. People' used to say to me' My word;l thought you would have been dead long ago.' I reslly thought so myself." "And do you mean to eav that this Improvement it awing solely to taking Dr Williams' pink pills r asked the reporter. "Certainly," was the reply. "I shall never be without them in the house. They are not purgative pills. I take one before retiring to rest every night, and you would not think what great benefit I derive the following moroing.l had often seen Dr Williams' pink pills praised in the newspapers, but it was not until this lady'per- sonally recomm ^nded them that I tried them. She told me that she had derived great benefit from taking them forlearpperisoning." Jackson,in reply to a further question,"l have no objection to you publishing a statement of my cure. I would be glad if others bens. filed by my experience." Mrs Jackson new appears to be a healthy looking woman of forty-six years. Her husband is in the ser- vice of the Ashton•in-Msherfield Rural District Council, and she has two eloildren—o daughter seventeen yeart of age, and aeon who is just two yearn younger.

Co-rioboratioa of this remarkable stateinteni was will- ingly given by a neighbour, a Mrs Derbi, shire, who has known Mrs Jackson for thirteen years. Mrs Derbyshire, although p a itieula rly busy, gave up a few minutes el her time on that market ay to answer a few questions put to her by the pressman. She contrasted Mrs Jack- eon's former condition with what it wa when this interview took place, and said that no one conversaat with the facts would doubt that her neighbour's cure is one of remarkable and exceptional interest.

WHEN DOES DEAFNESS BECOME INCURABLE.

Although great advances have been made in the treatment of Deafness in the past twenty-ive years, it has remained fir the Drouet Institute to modify the almost universal opinion, that long standing oases of Defective Hearing are incurable. In this respect, the report of twenty-pine cases cured by the Drouet Method during February, is truly mprising, RR a large number of the pa lents had been ',Meted by Deafness of lung daration and had previously tried Other epecial treatment wi,hout receiving definite benefit. Some of them, in- deed, had been operated on. In contrast with these failures, the success of the Drouet Treatment is so great that the Editor of the " Journal fors the D,af," has published details of these twenty-nine cases in the form of a " Supplement." A letter of thanks written by one of the patients is quite illus.,rative of the results reported in the other 28 cases. Nr. Ru4corabn Farm, Ruscornbe, Nt. Stroud, Gins., Feb. 13, 1902.

D ,ar Sir,—l have Iren a sufferer with Deafness f9r I some considerable time, and have been under treatment with several physicians, but it has all been to no effect, until I wrote the Drnuot Institute. Before I had been under that treatment long my hearing returned, and I can now hear quite well.—l remain, your faithfully, ARTHUR CHANDLER.

'Chose who desire to compare their own affliction with somo one of the cases reported in the " Supplement" can obtain a copy of this publication, with the "Journal for the Deaf," tree by pose, by a ldressing the Secretary, Drouet Institute, Regent's Park-road, London, N W. . . . .

Earth journal is accompanied by a Patient's Report Form which enables appicants to receive full particulars for applying the treatment at home. Any advice by correspondence is absolutely free.

THE WORK OF TEM GOVERNMENT.

The business part of the meeting having been con- cluded, an address was held by Mr J B Bottomley, of the National Union, London, At the outset be expressed regret that many so called Conservative Clubs were not of that real Oenefit ti the p nly thet they ought to be. Re conteviderl that the members of any clubs, whether Liberal or Conservative, should at least have some ele- mentary idea as to political work, the origin of political parties, and the work done by the parties, The present Government bad been described as ignorant- apathetic, impotent, blundering, dishonest, and bar- barous. It bad aloe been said it was the worst Govern- ment of modern times. Be desired not only to disprove that, but to prove that it was one of the beet Govern- ments they had had for a very long time. The Government came out at the last election in response to the challenge of the other side, and went back with a majority increased from 128 to 134. it was said they ' got their majority under false pretene,es by saying that the war was over. People new had their eyes ripen it was said, and if an election took place there would be an enormous difference of opinion. Ths r=i was one way of _

testing that, and it was by the by-elections. There had 21 by-elections; since the General Election, and the Government was stronger now than before the elections took place. The speaker alluded to Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman as the "so-called" leader of the party, and contended that the Heuel of Commons w4s stronger than it had ever been. Woy waS the Government so strone ?—because it was an honest Goyerromeat, a sum;easful Government, and he believed that if A dissolution were to take place to-morrow ney would have in the Country a repetition of what took place at the last general election. It was true that the Government had not done all it promised to do, toot it hal done more in a given time than any other Ministry which they could call to mind. The Government bad been greatly obstructed, and there were no fewer than 430 divisions last year in the House of Commons, that was to say toe tl4 days of , eight houro each were spent in walking through, the division lobbies. On the subject of the war he admitted that mistakes bad been made. hut there would be no Isdijaba nettleatent of the cam- nip with the present government in office. (Applause). He contended that it could be proved from the des- patches of Earl Roberts and Lord Kitchener that the Govntiment had given those generals everything that they had asked for. In conclusion the speaker urged upon his; hearers not to be over confident, but to work as if they had the strongest possible enemy to contend with. (Applause). ANAEMIA CURED

.ANCASHIRE.

A typical Lancashire working-class home is that of Mr. Kay, at No. 101, Bolton Old-road, Atherton; Mr. Kay is a miner. He has two stalwart POWS in the Army. One is in the Guards. Both are at the front. His daughter, Annie, a good looking girl of seventeen, although she would pass for several years older, works in the carding-room of the Atherton Co-operative Spinning Company s mill. A special importance attaches to the story of the girl, which induced a reporter to cal/ upon her to hear it from her own lips, the story of what was perhaps an escape from early ,4 death, thanks to Dr. Williams' Pink Pills fo Pale People. "About twelve months ago," Miss K " gen, "I became very ill. I had terrible pains in the stomach and head, and was -itkr pa 1e and weak. 1 had to leave my work at the mill and take to bed. My mother callenn a doctor, who prescribed for me, and I took his medi- cine, but it did me no good." "How long were you ill P"

"Six weeks. I did not take much of the doctor's medicine. He told me tha one of the causes of my illness was the defective state of my teeth, and so I went to the dentist's. After that I did get a little better, and returned to my employment in the mill, but it was not long before the old symptoms returned, and I was as bad as ever.' " Did you remain at the mill many weeks P"

"Two or three months. I called in another doctor. He told me I was suffering from anremia and ulceration of the coats of the stomach. It was both very serious and very pain- ful. I was in bed for another six weeks. The doctor called every day I took bottle after bottle of medicine, and his treatment finished up with a box of pills. He was a very clever doctor. While I was in bed under the second doctor a friend at Leeds came on a visit to us. Then I could eat very little, I slept badly, and my strength seemed to have left me altogether. She said that during the time she had been at Leeds she had suffered from fainting fits, and that she had been advised to take Dr Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. She did so, and three boxes completely cured her. She advised me to try the same. I took her advice. I discontinued the doctor's medicine and went on with Dr Williams' Pink Pills in- stead I very soon began to feel better. I got well gradually. By the time I had taken the first two boxes the pains in my head ceased, my stomach no longer troubled me, and the indi- gestion left me for good ; I could eat well and sleep better ; and was so far recovered that I resumed my work at the mill. I took six boxes a)together, and then gave over, because I was quite well, and stronger and heartier than I had been for two or three years."

THAT SUFFOCATING FEELING.

"That suffocating feeling." How many know it,how many feel it, after toe sligntest exertion ? The breath comes fitfully, there is pain in the side or in the chest, everything swims before the blurred eyes, and with a choking, suffocating feeling, you stand terror-stricken, for it seem like death. In nine cases out of ten it is not e:lue to the hear; but to the kidneys. Mrs E Carter's cure illustrates this. "1 bad a terrible pain at my heart," she says, in her own way, " its action being very irregular, which caused me to suffer from shortness of brts ath and palpi- tation. LOCAL NEWS

A Lancashire Worthy's Death, The death took place on Thursday morning at his residence, Whittle street, Little Hutton, near Bol- ton, of Thomas Whittle, eighty-one, after a few days' illness. Ile was appointed sidesman at the local parish church last Thursday. For forty years he was a teacher and master of Peel schools, Little Hutton, succeeding his father, who came from War- rington Grammar School. For nearly half a cen- tury he has been secretary to Little Hulton Penny Club.

Leeds City Council on Wednesday noted £5,000 to the Lord Mayor for the local celebration of the Coronation' Alderman Gordon protested against what he called the " compulsory loyalty "implied in the charge on the rates.

JOHN 'WALKER & CO.

EXANINER OFFICE, BEWSEY PTREET WARRINGTON. PRINTERS & BOOKBINDERS.

A gentleman who has the The Critics and the Will. disposition of six millions of property ought to be able to make his last testament interesting; but Mr CECIL RHODES has dose something more than that. He has embodied his views of the philosophy of life within a legal document, and in fact, used it as his first and last contribution to the world's literature. It is an instructive revelation of char- acter, and shows that in his heart Mr RHODES was better than he was always able to prove in his actions. The qualifications which he sets forth in his gift of scholarships remind one of Browning's lines," All I could hope to be." That horrible phrase, "Every man has his price," which Mr RHODES used in special reference to company pro- moting and the power to influence Park-lane created a very unpleasant impression at the time it was uttered. It had, however, the merit of candour, and this Empire-builder, though he never professed to have reached a high moral , standard, was consistent in the wish to inculcate its importance in others. "Truth, cour- age, devotion to duty, sympathy for the protection of the weak, kindliness, unselfishness, and fellow - ship," these are points to be reckoned in the race for honours and esteem. In this connection it is perhaps well to face boldly the question of the Raid in the light of a conversation which Mr. RTIODES had with Mr. HESS soon after that event. Mr. Rnones,according to the editor of the "Critic,' made it evident that he was really no party to "Dr. JIM" crossing the frontier, but, that mistake having been made, the Doctor could not be given away, in view of his coming trial. We hope that "Dr. Jim" will have something to say on this sub- ject now that the disclosure has been made. It is a courageous, although not always a wise, thing to stand by the indiscretions of others ; and, con- sidering how Colonel PRANK RHODES was involved in this affair, it is not unlikely that Mr. Czar. RuonEsl,thought it would be mean to disclaim. It is characteristic of the man that while appre- ciating learning so highly, he should deplore, in good-humoured satire, the unbusinesslike habits of the learned. The Oriel College authorities, he said, are "so like children in commercial matters.' It was as if the testator wanted to clearly show balance of judgment in his philosophy of life. He wished to emphasise that learning,for its own sake, was just as futile as the acquirement of riches for their own sake. These learned men, for whom be had such profound respect, in many ways were helpless except they were aided by worldly wis- dom. In CECIL REODES'S mind, a rich man who did not interest himself in culture, and imposed on the public by a gilt edge, was beneath contempt. For Society, with the capital S, Mr RHODES had no regard. He preferred the ,companionship of the animals in his Zoological gardens, and if there were any word he disliked more than another it was "smart." Mach will be said about the exemplary disposi- tion of Dalham Hall—the determination that there shall be no " loafers joined to the appre- ciation of the county gentleman as a factor of great importance in the English community. Then there is the gift of £lO,OOO to a distant cousin, a janitor in a bicycle store in the United States, who had wanted to join him in South Africa, and had been told curtly there were enough Rhodes theie. Mr. RHODES was determined, 111 running his scheme of a feierated South Africa, that it should rise:superior to any suspicion of family ambitions—the Hotel Cecil idea did not commend itself to him. For all that, however, he did not forget his kinsfolk. He recognised the personal obligation. The testament which Mr. RHODES has left is certainly a magnificent monu- ment to his memory, and confirms the opinion that his ambition in life was to be a benevolent despot. ' The benevolence is very marked ; the arbitrary will of the man is seen in the choice of Oxford to the exclusion of Cambridge, and in several other points, notably in the bequest for Gormaa students. CECIL Rnones was a man who would have his own way. But that, of course, was 00 secret of his success.

THE question may Rating of Machinery, well be asked what is the use of Royal Com- Missions if the Government treat findings with such scant courtesy as Mr LONG exhibited on Wednesday night? The recent Commission on local taxation reported very strongly in favour of the Rating of Machinery Bill, which came before the House of Commons on Wednesday in as com- Pact a form as possible. Mr LONG, however, gave the measure no encouragement, but frankly said that if a second reading were obtained the Government could afford it no facilities. He gave the old excuse that the time of the session Was fully mortgaged. Considering the strength cf the Unionist party in the House it should be practicable for Mr BALFOUR to mancsavre a few one-page Bills through the Commons .in aduition to the half-a-dozen larger schemes to which he is committed. The law in regard to the rating of machinery as at present applied is extremely confusing, and most inequitable. The ideal system from the T-4 iberal Point of view would undoubtedly be a change by Which all machinery used in manufactories should he released from all rating, because the principle of Liberalism has always been to leave inautifac- turers as unfettered as possible in their stern con- flict to win markets in the world. But such a sweeping alteration is not possible. By a strict interpretation of the law as it at present exists an assessing authority is bound, in calculating the ; val ue of manufacturingpremises,to take into account tiet only the value of the building itself, but what additional value is given by the machinery con- tained within the building. By a great many rating authorities this law has never been enlor2ed, and the result has been the growth throughout the country of an equitable and anomalous system by which manufacturers in one district find their machinery rated while those in ' another exempt from this burden have an unfair adv antage. The law as regards immov- able machinery is applied with practical • Unanimity all over the kingdom, and the Opposition to Mr. CuarmaN's Bill, which is to free . unfixed machinery, comes from mine owners and others, who must under any circumstances be subject to the extra assessment. A paint which should tell hi favour of the Bill is that the law of this country has always aimed at rating real pro- Party as against personal property, and movable - Machinery is emphatically the personal property of : the ma nufaoturer. Further, if movable machinery is to be taxed, as some authorities tax it now, Where is the application of the principle to stop? Is a brewer to be taxed upon the barrels Which he has upon his premises, and on the vats Which are part of the machinery of his business ? - Is the manufacturer who employs sewing machines to be taxed upon these? By an expan- sion of the argument, which :makes it logically absurd, the question arises whether the lawyer is to be taxed on his library, or the writer on the Pens which he writes with. It is impossible to aay what machinery should come within the law, and what be outside, and a real grievance has been created in districts where the law has been pressed into full operation. Mr. CHAPMAN'S Bill would Place manufacturers in all parts of the country °Tithe same basis, and as an act of justice it is urgently demanded. Let us hope that the Govern- Ment may still be induced to give the matter con- sideration.

A. correspondent who has been sending from the Persian Gulf to one of the London morning pApers alarm. ist messages with regard to the intentions of Russia gives incident ally some ludicrous examples of Turkish misrule on the Arabian shore. There are, for instance, some curious anecdotes of Turkish quarantine. Papers intended for the British telegraph clerks, for instance, at Busrah, the newly-arrived traveller has to hand to a Turkish official, who stands between the strangers and the clerks as if to ward off infection, takes the despatches gingerly between finger and thumbs solemnly dusts them with some kind of disinfect- ant, and passes them on to the proper recipient. It is the Turkish official's characteristic to perform these little tasks with a degree of scrupulous care not to be equalled anywhere else ; while idle dred- gers, and gunboats without ammunition and with- out coal rotting on the mudbanks, speak elo- naently of the greater duties left undone. But Tarkish nature is much the same everywhere. In Palestine, when an English firm with great gener- osity offered at its own expense to provide ought-stricken and fevered Jerusalem with a Water supply, the first reply was a refusal, and the aecond a delicate hint that on the payment of 4100 to the Turkish Governor the Englishmen nlight be permitted to perform the work they clEsired. As another instance of inept adminis- tration, it is reported that already th 3 road which WkS made through the country for the visit of the Grinan Emperor hardly three years ago is almost Obliterated, and its bridges broken and shattered. itch goverament might be expected to collapse of itself, but Turkey, from the first lodgment of the Ottoman power in Europe, has always been the tzception to every economic ' rule.

-- The lateness of the spring is a matter of corn- Mon talk. In another week we shall he half-way through the month of April, and neither in field laer in wood is much promise of the coining summer yet to be seen. In favoured dells, no doubt, the hawthorn bushes have that delicate arid transparent tint of green which is one of the Most charming sights of early sluing, but trees for the most part still show a mere black tracery against the sky. Tl:e elms should be in blossom now, giving, as the authorities on bee-keeping tell Us, the first fresh food which the inhabitants of , the hives will touch; but in most places the bees , i will have to keep up their life and their spirits a r little longer on the stored sweets of last year, for ' ..•1 generally speaking, the elms are almost as bare as 1 ,1 in the depths of winter. A more noticeable , , 'feature of the season is the lateness of the prim- rose, which in very few localities is more than , ' half out; indeed, unless milder weather sets in suddenly there may be but a scant supply of the - Dale flower for its commemorative uses upon , Primrose Day—a contingency which cannot be • . contemplated without a thrill of horror. The compensation for a late spring lies in the reflec'cion 1 • , that for some reason or other a postponement of ~ the budding season seems noW a regular sequel to ' a mild winter. Despite ono or two short snaps of tlld, and one which lasted long enough to give „ :scree gratification to the skater, frost in the dark 'oaths has interfered little with employment. As With warm weather leaves and flowers will soon Illake up for a rather bad start the penalty is not a ' E3vere one. , 1 ---o.—. MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

-- It will be gratifying to Parish Council members to know that in their several spheres they will be able to render the Coronation celebrations memor- able in the annals of the country. Certainly their power to spend public money is somewhat circum- scribed, but it will come to them and to the public as welcome news that Mr Walter Long has decid 3d to include the former in the Order to be ismed sanctioning the meeting ot the expenses of local celebrations out of the rates Thus pariM Conn- Cis in every part of the provinces will be enabled to give clay or Sunday school children a treat, hold sports,or light bonfires and bescmS, or do anyt ,ing in reason to celebrate the crowning of King Edward and his Qu-en. This is a real advantage, though it is hoped that local and private liberality will in all places supplement the rates,

The extent of the disaster at Glasgow has per- haps not even yet been told, as there remain many critical cases in the Glasgow hospitals. One of the remarkable circumstances in connection with the disaster, however, was the celerity with which accommodation was provided in the Western Infirm- ary for the reception of a hundred and forty patients. 'Nearly all the beds in the institution had been occupied before the stand collapsed. Rut the staff at once grappled with the difficulty and fitted up the necessary couches. The Western Infirmary I at Glasgow is famous in the records of surgery; for it was within its walls that Lister discovered, and first applied, the principles of antisepticism, under the influence of which surgery made so remarkable a progress during the latter half of the Victorian era. According to latest information the circum- stances leading up to the disaster are to be subject- ed to the most searching inquiry. The initiative lies with the Procurator-Fiscal,who is equivalent to the representative of the Crown in England, In Scot• land the Procurator-Fiscal inquires into all cases of suspicious deaths, although the coroner's inquest is really unknown beyond the Tweed. Tne Procura- tor-Fiscal will inquire into this matter, and has ' already charged experts with the examination of the faulty stand in Ibrox Park, and it is their duty to report upon the stability of the structure. After this a public inquiry will be held to satisfy public feeling. The accident, coming just now, should enforce a sense of responsibility upon those who at no remote period will be called upon to certify to the stability of numerous temporary structures for the accommodation of large and dense crowds, The searching examination of tAmporary structures seems to be all the more nec,s••ary when it is con- sidered that the Lbrox Park stand was a permanent one, that had passed thtough several fairly severe tests.

It must be fairly sati-factory to people in the provinces to know that the other evening the Government Licensing Bill passed its second read- ing with practically very little opposition. It was supported by tee Temperance Party on the sound an 1 safe Drinciple that half a loaf is better than no bread. The Archbishop of Canterbury, than whom there is no better temperance man, spoke in its favour. It was recommended by the Church of England Temperance Society, and found friends in the Councils of the United Kingdom Alliance. As for the Licensed Victuallers, they decided not to oppose the second reading, but to await the Com- mittee stage, when they will endeavour, by moving amendmerts, to obtain better terms than the Bill would grant them.

There is scarcely room here to recapitulate its provisions, but it may be well to state that the motive of the Bill is confined exclusively to the pre- vention of drunkenness, the penal laws in connec- tion with which are to be increased in stringency. Tnis applies to both male and female drunkards,and particularly of the habitual class. The Bill, very properly, proceeds, or appears to proceed, on the principle that some people get drunk on licensed ' premises, where also liquor is served to persons occasionally who are already intoxicated. The res- ponsibilities are therefore added to, not with any desire to harass. but with the object of securing the very desirable object indicated.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

A LOCAL PRIZE WINNER. At the coach% dug rounds in the tournament played in the Smelt Concert Hall, St George's Hall on Saturday evening, the second prize (.£2) in the ladies' open competition, was won by Mrs Mac- Lennan, Runcorn, that lady just missing the fitst prize by only one stroke. HoPE HALL MISSION. In connection with the Hope Hall Mission an interesting address entitled " Buy your own cherries" was given by Mr Blinston and his party of Widnes. The reading was illustrated by limelight views, the lantern being in charge of Mr Jones Mr Strange presided at the organ, votes of thanks ringing the meeting:to a close. A WOULD-BE SUICIDE. At the Runcorn Police Court on Wednesday, Joseph Gould waq charged before Mr G 11 Davies, with having attempted to commit suicide.—The facts showed that the prisoner alter loitering for some time near the Swing Bridge over the Ship Canal, jumped under the bridge with the obvious intention ot committing suicide. He was rescued with difficulty and on being asked why he had ' jumped into the canal wildly replied that he had better be sent to a hospital as they were trying to drown him.—Prisoner was remanded to Knutsford for seven days for medical examination. It was afterwards ascertained that the prisoner was a stowaway from the steamer " Fulwood," secreting himself at Bray, and was not discovered until the vessel reached Runcorn. RTNCORN WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION, The annual meeting of the above was held in Balton-road Schoolroom on Wednesday. Mrs Harry Baker presided over a good attendance of members. The officers and committee for the ensuing year were elected :—Mrs H Baker, president ; six ladies as vice presidents; Miss Emily Simkin, treasurer; and an executive committee of eleven girls, thus having one representative from each denomination in the town. During an interval refreshments were served. Songs were rendered by Miss Emily John- son and Miss Bazley. The Society has now a mem- bership of 196.

EARLESTOWN YOUTHS' PRI3AUCH• Four Earlestown youths, Adolphus Zorn, Walter Brown, Robert, I,:dwards, and Arthur Williamson,on the night of February 22nd last broke into the shed attached to the Masonic Arms, Newton, and stole five dozen half-pintpottles of stout and four dozen half-pints bottles of bottled beer, the pro- perty of Margaret Blundell, and valued at 28. On Thursday they were brought belore the Newton magistrates, who decided that Robert Edwards would have to go to gaol for oue month with hard about', Adolphus Zorn for fourteen days' hard labour, and Walter Brawn and Arthur Williamson Would each be fined 20s and costs.

GOOD TEMPLARY. The Hope of Earlestown Lodge meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall,Wargrave-road,on Monday, Bro Cutler C.T. presiding. Bro Wood L D read the Digest of No 1 convention to be held at Leigh. Bro Lindon PDM V D, briefly addressed the Lodge and pointed out the fact that the country's drink bill had been reduced by more than 2s per head of the population which he considered was mainly due to the temperance Crusade of the Free Church Councils and to the large increase made by the Good Templars. Resolved to pay a visit to the Pioneer Lodge, St lielens. One member was re-admitted and one proposed. A paper" Drink and Gambling" written by Bro Wakefield, was read by Bro:Woods in which the twin evils were graphically portrayed,the writer giving several incidents he cad personally witnessed, and deploring the extent which the evils had grown to in Earlestown. The paper was well discussed by Bros Woods, Hatton, Webster, Williamson and Newman, and on the motion of Bro Woods a vote of thanks was heartily accorded Bro Wakefield. The Lodge was closed the usual manner b y Bro Cutler C.T.

THE CHAIRSIAN THANKED FOR HIS SERVICES. Mr S DAVIES said he was reminded that that was the last meeting of the Boarci'a year and the} were again called upon to give praise or otherwise to the gentleman who had presided over their deliberations during the past twelve months. He was bold enough to say, and he thought no member would deny it, that the business of the Board had been presided over by the Chairman with tact and judgment of a very rare kind. His ruling could always be relied upon. Mr Jolley had so closely studied the working of the poor law that they could rely on his judgment and the Board had the utmost confidence in him. He ventured to think—and he did not say tus with any disrespect to former chairmen— that they had not had a chairman since the chairman- ship of Mr Edward Sleekly, who had discharged the duties so ably as Mr Jolley had done. It had been said that Mr Jolley was arbitrary at times and that when im- portant subjects came before the Board he had tried to stifle discussion and shelter himself behind the standing orders. THE CLERK'S BOND.

The regulations require the Clerk to enter into a bond with two sureties for the due performance of his duties. Upon is appointment in 1872,the late Mr Beamont and the late Colonel McCorquodale became sureties—upon the decease of Mr Beamont the late Mr Robert Davies became surety, with Colonel IdeCorquodale. From the decease of the Colonel, Mr Davies continued sole surety. —The Clerk now submitted the names of Mr Vere Beamont Davies and Mr Edward Birkconnel,who Would each enter into a bond for £5OO as sureties. FUNERAL OF THE LATE MR. W. MASSEY.

-- - On Saturday amid almost universal manifesta- tions of regret the remains of the late Mr W Massey were conveyed to their last resting place, the Run- corn Cemetery. The deceased gentleman though departing this life at the early age of 38 had endeared himself to the hearts of the majority of his townsfolk. He was an energetic yet unostenta- tious worker and in many •spheres of work, social political and religious, tie played a prominent part. Born in Runcorn amid humble surroundings William Massey rose to his respected posi- tion by determination and industry. When a boy he entered the office ot Messrs Simpson and Davies and steadily worked onward until he attained the responsible position he held at the time of his death. Born and bred a Wesleyan he'had continuously at heart the welfare of the con- nection in general and St Paul's Church aud Bruns- wick Sunday echool in particular. In connettion with the Church he held at various times several important offices each of which he filled with credit to himself and satisfaction to his superiors. In toe Sunday sahool he was beloved by his scholars. In their welfare he continuously took the keenest interest. He was one of the founders of Brunswick School Boys Brigade and supported the movement financially as well as morally. His greatest work, however, was for the cause of Odd- fellowship. Joining the Rose of elsaron Lodge or Grand United Order of Oddfellowe be early became officially connected with the order and was, in due course unanimously elected secre- tary—a post which he filled for over fifteen years with distinguished ability Possessing a rare ad- ministrative grasp, and having a peculiar genius for finance, he threw the whole of his energy in the interest or the order and worked early and late to ensure success. The rounding of the Juvenile and Ladies' Club is priacipally due cb the untiring efforte of the late William Massey and he has idt behind him a memory which will not sully be effaced in the minds of the • people of Runcorn For some time the deceased had been in failing health, active employment having to be relinquished some few weeks ago. He leaves a wife co mourn his decease. The funeral procession was formed at the deceased's late residence, 21, Victoria-road, and was perhaps the largest which has been seen in Runcorn for some time. The three Ledges comprising the Helton and Runcorn district were represenzed by nearly 200 officers and members, and besides these teem were representa- tives of the Brunswick Sunday School, and the principela and staff of Messrs Simpson and Davies. The names of the officers of the various lodges in attendance were :—Ro,se of Sharon Lodge : Messrs E Jones, Cele. Ratter and W Lambert (trustees), J E Jones and Samuel Bloor (auditors), T Davies (secretary ), J P uglas (treasurer), T Rogerson (sick visitor), John Hindley, jun., P.G., John Seeley, N.G., I' A Jones, V.G., J J Banner and L Bebbingtoa (guardians). V L Asucroft (warden), P Thompson (eol- lector). Rising Sun Lodge : Masers J Faulkner, J U B etworth and Walter Moore (trust-es). W H Bradbury and II Bowe (auditors), L J Eleighes, N.G. J E Deacon, V.G., Samuel Dawson, P.G., J Hancock (treasurer), R Hughes (secretery), W Moors (assistant secretary). Sleir of Freedom Led.e3 : Messrs G Hezlehurst (trustee), J H Doug as and W Lea (auditors), A Snaw (treasurer), W *Won (secretary), W Bennett Jones (assistant secretary), 0 Thom.s, VG. From the office rut Maasrs Simpson and Davies there were ;—Messrs J L Davies and Alfred Davies (princinals). and the following members of the Mice e,aff :—Messre Samuel C Beekett, E Taylor, J E Meredeth, C Po wnall, H Rowe, W Williamson, A Ridge, W I Millington, J Higgins, Geo. --*.,...... RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENTI Mr Wood raised an interesting point at the Board of Guardians on Tuesday. Two youths sent from the Runcorn Union to a training ship have recently received their discharge and the Guaruians have been notified of the fact. Thus the matter might have ended had it not been decided, owing to one or two pertinent questions, that the clerk institute in- quiries BO that the future career of the Youths may be reported upon periodically to the Union. One would think it was a matter of the utmost interest to ascertain whether the youths were proving in at terlife a credit to themseives.and to those who first were instrumental in placing their I feet on the lowest rung of the ladder.

Murmurs of disgust and astonishment per- vaded the Board Room when Mr Woodward read out the cost of vaccination for the last three months, The actual figures are given in another column and it is stated that the cost really means a charge upon the rates of at least one penny in the X. This should be very cheering news both to vaccinator and anti-vaccinator. Still the Board ought not to grumble. 'Pis but a few weeks since they passed a resolution against those opposed o vaccination and which would almost have the I effect of compelling all " to make bare their arms." Necessarily an increased rate is the result. The Guardians really ought to be pleased at the first fruits of their policy.

It has yet to be decided whether the man who tears his coat on a fence can claim from the owner of the fence or from the local authority,who should see that the fence is kept in proper repair. The Raral Council are confronted with this point and its negotiation involves several pretty isues. It is of direct importance to the public, and when the result is pronounced an important decision will have been arrived at which should interest everyone.

The Urban Council's estimates for tha year will be found in another column. Ratepayers will learn with interest that the General District Rate will be 3s. 6d. for the coming year. It seems rather heavy but then a town cannot have a park, leafy avenues, with flower garden improved streets, pros- pective baths and an energetic body of officials to to keep the town from running to seed, without haring to pay for such u:eful luxuries. Instead of grumbling about the amount, which is a verit- able trifle compared with other towns, house- holders should be only too pleased to pay their quota and be devoutly thankful that it does not cost them a couple of shillings more in the .6.

One of the meanest and most contemptible thefts ever noted by the recording angel came to light at the Runeorn Police Court the other day. A poor old man of seventy worn and bowed with age, had just received the modest:sum of four shillings from ' the:parish authorities to keep soul and body together when a woman—pardon the name—with her chil- dren entered his house and hypocritically asked whether there was anything she could do for him. The old man, taking pity on the ragged little children, gave bar one of his precious two shilling pieces and asking her to purchase a few humble necessaries of existence. The woman clutched the coin and departed and she did not re-appear until late in the evening—drunk with the money she had stolen. Bereft of his money the old man neither ate nor drank on Saturday and Sunday and on Tuesday had to be removed to the Workhouse. The woman whec arrested positively gloried in her crime and to the magistrates order of a month's imprisonment shamelessly bawled out that she could do that terra an her head " Very good, then," replied the chief magistrate." You shall go to Kuutsford for two months instead of one." The woman sullenly left the dock somewhat sobered by the judicial repri- mand.

SUNDAY OBSERVANCE.

A Bishop's Heterodoxy.

-- The Vicar of Frodsham has obtained an episcopal ally. Speaking at Norwich Diocesan Conference on the question of Sanday recreation, the Bishop of Ipswaich said it was known how the rich spent their Sunday on the river, and they had no excuse for it,but the working people had to work from Six CO EiX, and they had no opportunity for recreation, except on Saturday afternoon. It would be well to look at the matter from top to bottom, and consider whether they should not revert .;() recreation between the hours of service. There could be nothing wrong in being happy on Sunday,. There could be nothin; wrong in keeping people out of temptation as Ireg ag public-t”uses were opened on Sundays. As soon as the church bell began to ring, the recreation—football in winter and cricket in summer— should be stopped, and he did not know that anyone would say his nralers the worse for having made a nice drive to the off.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH, WARRINUTON. A Successful Sale of Work.

Success again attended the annual sale of work in Connection with St Paul's Church, Warrington. This function has now become a very popular event and Monday's proceedings gave evidence of this fact. The schoolroom in Kendrick-street looked bright and attractive with a number of stalls containing plain and fancy goods, confectiouey flowers and plants, and the space allotted to the public was crowded with willing purchasers. Shortly atter half-past two, the opening ceremony was performed by the Hon Miss Ellinor Wilson- Patten. The Rev '3 R Tarbuck (vicar of St. George's, Altrincham) and late vicar of the parish. presided and he and Miss Ellinor Wilson-Patten were accompanied on the platform by the Ron. i Elizabeth Wilson-Patten, the Mayoress (Mrs Parr), Mrs Lyon, Mies Juliet Macdonald, Mr Daubeney,the Rev W Bracecamp, vicar-desigisate of St Paul's, Mr R J Ceorley (vicat's warden), Mr J B. Saaethurst (people's Warden), and Messrs J Richardson, Bur- gess, and Lawton (sidesmen), While in the body of the room tee following clergymen were also pre- sent :.—The Rector aud the Revs BC E Carleton, H pigot, W Pollock-ilill, and L W Thomas The following is a list of the scallbolders :—No. 1 stall, Mesdames Gibson Smith, R J Chorley, Lord, Worrell, and Miss D Reid. Number 2 stall, Mrs Reps herd, and the Misses Geddes. Number 3 stall, Mesdeme, Tarbuck, Bracecamp, Thomas, J H srnethurst, Lindley, Lawton, r Locker, and Mass Pierpoint. Number 4 stall Mesdames burgess, Hankinson and Conon. Number 5 Still, Mrs J W Parker, and the Misses Finney and Gator. Number 6 stall, Mrs Sherlock and the Misses Craik, Ashurst, and Lyttlgoe. The Rev E R TAIIBUCK, at the outset, said they were rather unfortunate in not having the Mayor %vita thorn to support the Misses Wilson-Patten, but he had to at :end a oornmittee meeting at the Town Hall at the same hour as the opening of the sale. He (Mr Tarbuck) had also to regret the fact that awing the sear a good friend of the parish had passed away in the person of kr Robert Davies , whom they all admired. (Hear, hear.) Another regret was that their old frienu Mr Alderman Sutton had had to leave the town on important busi- ness, and would not be able to propose the usual vote of thanks to the lady opening the sale Then the Rev S Wilkinson, a former vicar of the parish had sent a letter regretting his in- ability to be present, and he was sure they would all be sorry that Mrs Beamont, a great friend to St Paul's, could not be with them. Speaking of the work of the parish, the rev gentleman said they had been very fortunate during the year. It laad improved con- siderably in every direction, and they would be able at the annual vestry meeting on the hollowing evening to show that the receipts in the church alone-had exceeded all past experiences, and amounted to no loss a sum than £4BO. (Applause.) When they coesidered that the parish was composed almost entirely of the working classes, those who had corns amongst them that day would see that the congregation were doing their best. (Applause.) Last year it was his privilege to write to this Hon Mrs Harbord to ask her to give them land on which to build a parochial hall and working men's in- stitute. With the greatest kindness she put the matter , into the hands of her agents, Messrs White and Co., and I the lady had kindly made over to them a very handsome , portion of land in the most suitable position they could have chosen, and they ware all greatly indebted to her for her magnificent gift to the parish. (Applause). Mrs Harbord was asked to open the sale, but for reasons with which they sympathized with her she found it im- possible to come to la arrington, and it suggested itself to him that they could not do better than ask the Hon. Miss Ellinor Wilsou-Patten to perform the ceremony and she consented to do so. (Applause). He had received a letter from Alderman Sutton, in which be alluded to the fact that there was no name so sweet in the ears of Warringtea men as that of Winmarleigh, or Wilson Patten, and it was pleasing to know that since Lord Wininarleigles death his interest in all Warringtou charitable instltutions and religious work had been carried onley the Misses,Wilson-Patten whom they gladly welcomed to the sale that day, as well as the Mayoress and Mrs Lyon. (Applause). The Hon. Miss Eeterion WILSON-FATTEN, in declaring the sale of work open, said she was very sorry that her niece. Mrs Harbord, could not be there that day. She should inform her niece that she had been very much missed, but as her husband was still in South Africa, and as she was looking from day to day to the time when he would return, they could scarcely expect her to leave London and come so far away as Warrington. She had great pleasure in declaring the sale open, and she believed Mr and Mrs Tarbuck and the rest of the people who had been engaged in getting it up would have the pleasure of seeing the stalls completely emptied of the beautiful things with which they were now laden. (Applause).

He asked his friends to help him in every way postible. He was pleased to see the Rector and several of the other clergy present, and he hoped they would all work together. lie was only coming to Warrington with one object in view, and that was to try to carry on God's work in his humble way, asking Him for k treogth. A DANGEROUS FENCE. .

The following. letter eated April Ilth, was received from Mr. E. V. Walker, editor of the Warrington " Examiner "—" I should be glad to hear what your Council proposes to do re my claim for compensation put in some weeks ago. It is quite time, I think, that any question of liability between the Council and the Ship Canal Company was settl.d. Soma information on the matter would be welcomed by me," Mr Swinton (surveyor) said be hacl communicated with one official who had not yet answered his letter. He bad found out that the fence belonged not to the Council, but to the Ship Canal Company. Mr Stokes: Another case of saddling it on the right horse ! (Laaghter.) I A Member: If the surveyor claims it is not ours we are not in any way responsible. If that is so we had better state to him that we are not responsible and that he had better apply to the right patty. Mr Woodward asked would they be doing a wise thing in so acting. The better course would be to wait the decision of the Ship Canal Company b-fore replying. The surveyor might be right but the proper course to pursue was to wait for a definite statement before finally settling on a course which might lead to litigation. He would like to know whether the fence was erected in conjunction with the Highway Committee and whether there was any provision which handed over the main- tenance of the fence to the Council. Mr Linak-r quite concurred in Mr Wool ward's view. Mr Pilling believed that some time ago the Ship Canal Company repudiated any liability in connection with the fence. Mr Linaker, in reply to a remark that it would be better to pay the uoat of the coat, said they bad no right to chuck away 35s of the ratepayers money, but at the same time it would be foolish to defy a person who bad a just claim against them.
