TERRIBLE DEATH OF A ST. HELENS MAN. Michael Cox,aged twenty-one,met with a terrible death at St Helens on Thursday. Cox was employ- ed at the Southport Collieries. At half-past three o'clock in the afternoon he went down with two other men to the mouthing of the nine feet mine. After having his lamp examined he turned round, but instead of going round the side he stepped right into the shaft and tell between the spars to the bottom of the pit, a distance of 250 yalds.

TRA ELLING VLORLNTS. A circular was received from the Newcastle-Under- Lyne Union with reference to the question of vagrancy especially when women end children travelled with atle bodied men from one Union to atother. Many vagrants were a prey upon unprotected females aud the occu- palate of isolated houses. Statistics showed that that class of vagrants was on the increase. The Neacastle Guardians considered that such tramps should be deprived of the control of children under fourteen years of age and that such children should te sent to indus- trial schools. It was requebted that the Runcorn Dis- trict Council pass a similar resolution and that copies should be sent to the Local Government Board. If the act were passed habitual tramps would dilapoear and children would be thoroughly protected 'Ail.: travelling workingmen would be protected. Referred to the Parliamentary Committee on the motion of Mr Woodward and Dr Gastalcil.

Warrington Hairdressers' Association.— Presentation to the Retiring President. . _

The annual- -ineeling took place at the Cocos Rooms, Horsemarket-street, when the chair was occupied by Mr McClew.—Before proceeding with the ordinary business an interesting and pleasing duty was performed by Mr Poimore, ten. The members of the Union, he said, had learned with deep regret that Mr McClew bad found it imperative, owing to failing health, to vacate the position of P-e.ident, which he had so ably filled since 1893, the year of the inception of the organisation. There was a unanimous desire to show appreciation of the good work done by their retiring President during his term of office, and he (Mr Podmore) rhad been requested, as the father of the Trade in the district, to present to Mr Nfc lew a silver cruet as a mark of the esteem in which he was held by his confreres. (Cheers). It was a small gift, but it carried with the goodwill of all, and a hearty wish that his itell-earned rest wonld restore him to robust health. were was one incident that ought to be made known. Mr McClew,Nieffedgcock,sen ,and himself were fellow-tradesmen forty years ago, when they inaugurated a movement in favour of Sunday closing. They induced the other hairdressers to fall in with them, and they decided to close on a certain Sunday. They remained closed on that particular day, but the great bulk opened again the week following. The thrqt of them, however, made a determination that once they closed their shops on the Sunday they would not open again on any aecount, and they had set an example which be was glad to say had been acted upon by the members of the union. Mr Fledgcock, sen., and Mr `-;raith (the Secretary) also spoke. The latter remarked that the glasses of the cruet had been specially cut by a Warrington tradesman. Mr McClew made a brief response. in which he heartily thanked the members for their sincere wishes for his return to robust health and for their annrecia- deal of his work as President. The beautiful testi- monial Ur Podmore had handed to him would, he could assure them, he highly treasured by himself and the whole of his family.

"THE SOCIALIST AND LABOUR MOVEMENT IN HOLLAND."

Lecture at Warrington.

A small audience gathered in tilri Druid& Elall,Sankey- street, YVarr neon, on Mon lay evening, to listen to a lecture by J C Van Her V. r. socialist writer, speaker ard exile (late of Holland). The subj ,ct of the lecture was The socialist and labour movement in Holland." Mr Stott presided and regretted that ttere was gush a small attendance. Whilst it was possible to get 60,000 or 70,000 at a great forstbaff match for a little more than as hour's excitement it was difficult to get a small number to listen to something which would be to their advantage. As socialists they w. re in sympathy with all who wsre oppressed. It did not matter syhether it was lb English workingman, the Irish peasant, the Scotch crofters, or the Welsh qaarrymen, or whethcr they were oppressed workers in India, the Colonies, or the Europesn States. They recognised that the interests of workingmen were the same the world over, and as soc alists they lest their asoistance and sympathy to those who were oppressed by the capitalist systsm. Tne socialist pa ti• had net I made that progress which rimy of them whirred. They had net a labour rerresentative en the Town Council or the Board of Guardians. The members of the Independ- ent Labour Palsy were datsrmined toga on with their educational work which had led many to be more toler- ant to S-cialists than they were some rears ago. M. J C VAN DER VEER then proceeded with his lecture. Hs spoke in broken English but he had no difficulty in making himself understood. At the outset he [rased the history eff Holland It war not until the beginning of last century, he said, that the voice of the people was beard demanding the freedom and independence which the middle-class enjoyed. Socialism, however, made little progress, and it: was not wail 22 years ago that a minister of religion named F Domela Nieuwenhuis, gave a needed stimulua to the movement. He left the church and although a member of an aristocratic family, he de- voted himself entirely to the preaching of soci dism. The cause spread rapidly and thr Goverment took nteps to suppress it, by persecuting tho leaders. Nieuwenlessis was tnrost into prison for les majestic. The lecturer a.r. oceedea to recount some of his own exp, minces in the movement. He him- self had been imprisoned no fewer than eight tilers for his socialist opinions. The persecution onls tended to make the socialist movement stronger and at the present time, the socialists were net only represented in Parliament but t ley had a daily paper of their own, a number of weekly socialist papsrs ond several maga- zines. Soeialiern,he contended, was the only cause that could bring aterot a real change I the condition alif the working classes. The evils of the pins int capitalist crit- ters were ts be seen in every country and the labour movement in Holland was really the saniaiss movement. There was no such difference between lab ur leaders and socialists as was seen in England. The people of X .Iland looked upon the English people as the pioneers of civilisation and independence and he knew something of the old spirit was lefc. He hoped English socialists would take heart from what the Socialists had been able to accomplish in Holland. He was sorry to see that the women of England did not take as much interest in socialism as the woolen of Holland. The wives of the working cit spa bad more to gain by the adoption ef socialism than anyone also. In conclusion he urged all socialists to renewed efforts. In answer to a question by Mr NIGHTINGALE, the LECTINtIen said the working classes of Holland read a great deal. They did not spend as muela money or time up10:1 sport as the English.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

Criticism by the Rev. J. F. Matthews.

Speaking at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday afternoon, the Rev. J. F. Matthews, pastor of Latchford Hap let Chapel. said he had usually confined himself, when visiting that P.S.A., to the die- cussiou of one or two phases of some momentous religious topic. At the present moment they were face to face with one of the most serious crises of the last 30 leers in our national history. He referred to the crisis treught about by the introduction of the Education MI, &bolt which he wished to say a word or two. f here was one thing in connection with the Bill with which be was in 'empathy. That feature was the attempt to unify the different, diverse and confused Systems of education under which the education of this country was at present conducted. We bad local Teclealhil Instruction Cemmitteee, Instruction C ,mmittees of the County Councils, School Boards, Science and Art Department, and the Board of Educa- tion ; aed all theee verious classes otreducation•l autho- rities worked independently: of each other. The new Education Bill tried to make one supreme authority in the "ducat onal forces of the country. So far so good, but unfortunately the grreit weakness of the bill wee the proposal to make the Ceanty fi'ourreel the educational authorte for the County, the Borough Council for tne County Boroughs and the Urban Dietrict Council frr urban districts. DO NOT THE WRONG. Do not the wrong that goofs may COMO Whatever others say, For all who would the world improve Must keep the narrow way. We cannot make the darkness less ley shutting out the light, The only read to happiness IR in the path of 114ot. If on the moving sands we build Rich temples broad and high, A moment they may dazzle us Then all in ruins lie. only on the rock of truth Where nations can be free, That we can huild pure joyful homes Of pesce and liberty. Not where ambition's selfish aim Is to affection blind, Nor where the eager love cf wealth Is narrow and unkind. Do not the wrong to gain a place Where all thy worth may see, Yet be deserving of toe beet Whatever Comes to thee. Do not the wrong, be not mislead It will thy peace destroy, And we have teed of all that gives To life its fullest joy. Do not the wrong, sow not the seed From it no flower can spring Avoid the bane of human life Weed out the cursed thing. Warrington April Mb, 1902. S. KENYON.

Ping-Pong Tourna—ment at Warrington. Despite the tact that the new game of ping-pong has many enthusiasts in Warrington, the tourna- ment which was held in Parr Hall on Saturday was set attended with great success. The promoters of the tournament had hoped it would be t)ae means of augmenting the funds of the Y.M.C.A., but unfortunately the function was not sufficiently sup- ported by the public to enable this deserving insti- tution to benefit at all. Doubtless the wretched weather which prevailed during the greater part of the day was to some extent accountable for the inarill attendance of spectators. The number of competitors did not come up to anticipations, but notwithstanding this, the games were contested with much zest. Three events were arranged— namely, for ladies, gentlemen,and juveniles,and the winners of the prizes are :—Gentlemen's Competi- tion.—First prize, £2 2s; second prize, Li Is : 1, Mr 0 W Heald ;2,Mr C Bishop. Ladies' Competition. First prize, £2 2s ; second prize, .£1 Is : 1, Miss E Barlow ; 2, Miss Thorburn. Juvenile Competition. —First prize, lOs ; second prize, 5s ; 1, Rix Field ; 1, It F Anderton. The arrangements for the tourna- ment were in the hands of Messrs P Garnett and P Bean and it is to be regretted that their praise- worthy efforts did not meat with the success which was to be desired.

SEQUEL TO AN ACCIDENT AT A

WARRINGTON WORKS.

A Workman Claims Damages in the County Court.

Before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands at the Warrington County Court on Thursday. last week George Berry, of 156, Manchester-road, Warrington, wirewarper and winder,claimed £4O damages under the Employers' .Liability Act, 1880, from Thomas Locker and Company Limited,ovire manufacturers, Ellesmere-street,for injuries received whilst in their employ. Mr F Cuthbert Smith, barrister, of Livers pool (instructed by Mr T S steel, of Warrington) represented the plaintiff, and Mr Aahead Elliott (instructed) by Messrs Chapman and Brooks of Mancheater appsared for the defendants. . _

At the outset, Ilitr Smith asked leave to alter the amount of ,damages to that of three yedr,' eardingol.

Mr Elliott said that was the first his clients bin heard of such a suggestion and be opposed the application, They had not come prepared to defend a larger claim. It might be necessary Co call a medical expert. Hie Honour said tie weind leave, the amount of dam- ages an open one, and it Mr Elliott desired Co call any witnesses who were not in Court be would giant au adjournment. Mr Smith then proceeded to prove the plaintiff'. case. The plaintiff, he said, had hem employed at Messrs Locker's for three or tour months when the accident happened on the 13th of September last year. He was employed on a loom near Co which stood a drilling machine. The drilling michine was driven by pulleys which were driven by belting. There were two pulley s cnuected with the reachires one of them being what was known as a free or loose pulley which revolved when the machiee was at a standstill and the other was a driving pulley which when set in operation supplied the power ,to work the machine. In order to trausfer the belting from the loose pulley to the driving pulley it alias netessary to pull a bar which bad a fork attached. The plaintiff alleged that the bar was defective inasmuch as it was short of a bolt kbieb used to catch a portion of the machine and prevent the belt from being pushed off the wheel entirely. The bolt or pin WAS missing and the result was that when toe drilling machine was being used the belt was pushed off the wheel. Mr Wood, who was the torrman, asked the plaintitff to leave the loom he was working and help him to pu the belt on the pulley again. The plaintiff did so, but on the day of the acciesnt the belt was pushed off again. The plaiutiff was once more asked to assist in putting the belt on again. It was while assisting Wood to do so that the belt caught the driving pulley and the machine was lifted bodily from the Itior. The foot of the machine caught the plain if on the shin of the leg and caused the injuries complained of. H. (Mr Smith) contended that the accident was due in the firs. place to the absence of the bolter pin in the rod which regulated the belting on the pulley Then he the second place, the machine ought 'ee have been properly bolted d ism so as to prevent it from lifting. The plaintiff endeavoured to resume work on :he day following the accident but he was obliged to seek the aid of a doctorand was attended by Dr Harcourt. On the 16th.the plaintiff's wife served a notice at tbe office of the flern,which read as follows: "3. Wilson-Patten-street, October 16th, 1901. I having attendance on George Berry, of 156, Menctiester-road, certify be has deep-seated trouble in the leg following injury received at your place and it will be some weeks before be is fit to return Cm his work.' &mall Hai court." Mr Elliott urged that no notice of injury had nem given by the plaintiff pursuant to 42 and 44 Victoria, chapter 42,seetioo 4.He contended that the letter of the medical man was not a notice at all and did not comply with the section or •the Employers Liability Act which regulated the notices in each cases. A long legal argunaeet aimed between Mr Smith and Mr Elliott and at the close, the Court aojeurned for '1 luncheon.

His Horour held the notice to be defective, but con- sidered it to be sufficient for the defendants if not sensing prejudice to them, and not being given for the purpose of misleading. _ . . _ _ _

1' lir Smith said be a-hould contend that before the action commenced there was an unqualified admission of liability on the part of the defendants. On the 11th February a 1-ttter was written by Ur Bland, the resident secretary of the Insurance Company for the defendants in Manchester, to -Mr Steel who was acting for the plaintiff. la the letter Mr Bland state 1 that he would be obliged if Mr Steel eould make an appointme, t for Mr Derbyshire, a repre- sentative of the firm, to wait upon him wi h reference to the matter oolany :date except Tuesdayior Thursday afternoons. Mr Steel replied on the 12th stating that be could see Mr Derbyshire at 3.30 on Friday afternoon, Between that date and the 17 h February, a representa- tive of the firm—be (Mr"Scnith) did not know whether it was Mr DarbyAhrre—arrived at the work.; where the plaintiff was trying to do a bit of wwk, accompanied by a contleinan who be (Mr Smith) believed was a doctor. Be had not been to see the solicit ir in accordance w,th the appointment made,he had not attempted to see him The company's representative saw the Plaintiff and made him a direct offer of £5. Plaintiff refused to accept it, whereupon the representative made use of language which he (Mr Smith) did not think his employers would have allowed him to have used if they bad known, and told the plaintiff that that was Rh lbe was likely to get and if be did nit tsk. £5 he would get nothing. Plaintiff told him that he must see his solicitor, and he replied," I don't want to see your solicitor. I am dealing with you siaiply." He (Mr Smith) said - that under the circumstances in which the course was taken by the people who represented the defendants, this was an admission on the part of tha defendants that they thought .hey ought to pay the man something, . . . .

Sinc,. the accident be had received from the fir -nlhe sums of £1 pr week for four weeks and 15e for four wHks. He worked for 99 of this amount.

House-cleaning

Ordinary soap—rubbing, scrub- bing, sweating—smell of soap suds, confusion, steamed windows, ruffled temper.

Fels-Naptha—easy as cleaning tea-things--stains disappear—dirt lets go its hold—dusting is, scarce easier. It's the naptha in the soap that does it.

As easy for you to prove as it is for us to say so. Your grocer sells yqu a bar for 2?,c1 on this condition that you get your money back if you're not satisfied.

Th toe topic of the moment is the annual, one of the Net, which is always an unenviable affair for a tha,"ee„llor of the Exchequer, but never more so lor-,4,1-e4-8 week, when Sir Michael Hicks-Beach had a0,,,`4e third time to ask for a record vote on `i'ollt of the huge expenses of the War. Nobody goti,,better aware than he, when he rose in the Thn Thursday, that the country, both and masses, were already taxed up to the er,z, tand paying more to the public purse than . wlore, while the long-drawn-out finish of the 40t'hall;1 the huge army still in South Africa did th SIP to lighten his task in any way, te9B t_ he was in a position of unpleasant- oh has hardly ever been rivalled in the of politics. However, of all the Cabi- tettebl:- ,1201- all the House, Sir Michael Hicks- IS pre-eminently the man to carry off

.N. SIB MICHAEL HICKS-BEACH, MX, Le as well as it can be done, as he is essen- fighting politician and a strong man. ,ed income tax he knew would rouse the ire classes, the backbone of the electorate,

augmented tax on tea or sugar he was well aware would not be popular with the classes. and so forth with each possible for taxation, but he is not the man to shrink 'hat he considers his financial duty on such • Budget always results in grumbling quarter touched by fresh taxation, and the ',Pillar Chancellors of the Exchequer are Qse pleasing, but rare duty it is to announce OrT4 us and consequent reductions on various .R„,of general consumption. Sir Michael !:ieach, however, is not in lack in this respect unparalleled deficits and constant calls on ,rse, but his opponents are amongst the the stoical way in which he grapples Ac. .t men have written books to ex-

live., to condone their mistakes, and :41 their motives to posterity, but not so le Mr. Cecil Rhodes. He simply made a .411 d a great will, which is at once an 'e and a human document, in which the inn stands unintentionally before the eyes gel,lowe, better to be understood in death life. Much of the bitter enmity he thS* years of his supremacy in be assuaged by the insight given into u• of Mr. Rhodes by ;hils vrill, and his -it as they were in soine

•

ilnerialism proper, the welding together of the I Mother Country and her Colonies by the special preparation and education of a certain number of the representatives of the latter in the tradi- tions of the former. Never a man to do anything stingily, his gift of Colonial and American scholarships, numbering one hundred and sixty- four, and amounting to some two million in gilt- edged securities, vies with Mr. Carnegie's big schemes, surpassing them in the fact that it is more cosmopolitan and calculated to aid a far more widespread class, while his fifteen founda- tions for Germans at the nomination of the Kaiser show that keen feeling of reciprocity which under- lies all true statesmanship. The will commences with directions for his burial in his favourite spot in the Matoppo Hills, which shows a Stevensonian view of sentiment, and provides benefits for Rhodesia and Bulawayo from his estates. Then comes a bequest of £lOO,OOO to Oriel College, Oxford, his old college, about which he character- istically advises these children in commerce "to consult his trustees. Then follow the sixty Colonial scholarships of £3OO a year each for three years, and the hundred and four American, and the fifteen German foundations, all to be awarded in a most carefully-thought-out way, which will help to combine natural brain-power, education, physique and moral character in their proper proportions. Groote Schuur, his beautiful residence near Cape Town, he has left to South Africa to be the home of the Premiers, and a large sum has been thoughtfully set aside for the up-keep of the estate in proper style and for the provision of an adequate staff. His various private bequests are not the business of the public, but interest is attached to the settlement of Mr. Rhodes's newly acquired Dalham Hall Estate, near New- market, which is left to Colonel Frank Rhodes and his heirs, with the remainder to Captain Ernest Rhodes for his heirs male, with the character- istic condition that no " loafer " shall succeed, and that each holder of the property shall serve ten years in some profession or busi- ness, and do certain military service. The total amount left is valued at six mil-

lions, and Lord Rosebery, Lord Grey, Lord Milner Mr. Alfred Beit Dr. Jameson, Mr. L. L.

Michell and Mr. B. F. Hawrksley are executors, trus- tees and tenants for life of residuary estate. It is, undoubtedly, a great will, and the will of a great man. which will go far to vindicate him with posterity. On Thursday a simple, but impressive memorial service was held at St. Paul's, and it was largely attended by all sorts and conditions of the dead politician's admirers.

It is doubtful whether any King of England ever spent a more delightful holiday than King Edward's yachting cruise round the South Coast, and it will certainly serve to set Ms Majesty up for the trying season in front of him and the ordeal of the Coronation and its festivities. During the earlier part of the time the Victoria and Albert, which is probably the most comfort- ably fitted out yacht in the world, was stationed at Cowes, but after Easter she moved from her

THE KING'S TIOLIDAY :

A SNAPSHOT OF HIS MAJESTY ON RI MOTOR OAR. anchorage and went ao,s:n the coast towards Cornwall and the West. In addition to his yacht, King Edward has been making use of his latest motor-car for land work, and many pleasant expeditions have been made from the coast tc inland cpots of interest. Of these the visit to Beau- lieau and the thirty-five mile drive through the New Forest on Mr. Scott Montagu's car was the greatest, and probably the most enjoyable, as the day itself was so gorgeous. Another place called at further down the coast, which must have filled His Majesty as much with sorrow as with interest, was Portland Prison, where the King saw six hundred of the convicts defile out to their work after dinner. The holiday, with ,its vazious little excitements and informal visits, has been as pleasant as each day was long, and the King must be glad that he decided to abandon his proposed trip to the Riviera and spent his vacation amongst his own people Instead, and in addition to his own personal rest and enjoyment, it has caused them in their turn real pleasure, and has served to familiarise him with parts of his own Kingdom nearest the capital which he could hardly have known very well before. Of the weather it can only be said that it might have been bett,er, but at the same time it might have been worse, and we all know the proverbial uncertainty of an English spring.

The object of each production at Drury Lane seems to be to out-Herod Herod and out-do all of its previous Brobdingnagian feats of stiage-oraft, and Ben liar is no exception to the rule. In fact, the great chariot-race is the greatest triumph of theatrical mechanics yet seen and ten years ago it would not have been deemed possible. On the stage at Drury Lane you can see an ancient Roman chariot-race from your stall going on before your eyes and readily be deceived into a sensation of reality. The horses gallop, the drivers drive furiously, the scenery passes swiftly ° before the ,yes of the audience, and it is all the time a mechanical "fake." The horses gallop, but never progress, the stage being moved under them by a mighty invisible mechanism, while the scenery is worked by the biograph's aid, itself moving and giving the effect of the chariots at full speed. Of all, the accident is the most realistic and forms an ad- mirable climax. But great as is the production

scUSEts ritom "BEN Hilt " AT DRURY LANE, AND THE AUTHOR OF THE GREAT PLAY. • and much as Mr. Arthur Collins it to be congratu- lated upon his ingenuity, it is doubtful whether it Will be a really big success, as there are many elemants in the play which tend towards failure. The ,storY is one of the best known ever written, owing to an almost unparalleled circulation, and therefore need not be referred td; it will suffice to say that this tale, dealing with Lthe actual days of the Messiah, scarcely lends itse f to adaptation for stage purposes, and, however ca:refully arranged and toned down, is apt to offend the susceptibilities of some people, who go so far as to class it as irreverent, though this is anything but the spirit in which it is intended. Mr. Robert Tabor plays the name part very satisfactorily, while in a big caste Miss Constance Collier. Miss Maud Milton, Mr. J. E. Dodson and Mr. Bucklaw dc particularly well. The 'new Hood and Gentian (a combination which is getting as regular as Gilbert and Sullivan • used to be) opera, Aterrie England. has scored a success at the Savoy, and promises tc do very well. The book is distinctly good, and the music excellent, and Mr. German is rapidly taking his place in the front rank of British composers.

SEQUEL TO AN UNSUCOSSEFUL ASSIZE ACTION.

Local Farmer in the Bankruptcy Court.

As the Warrington Bankruptcy Court, on Friday, before Mr Registrar Ridgway, Matthew Pimblett Pow- nail, residing and carrying on business as a fara.er at Brookside Farm, Sankey, appeared for his public examination. The liabilities were expected to rank at £B4l 18s, the assets being estimated to realise £155 18s 2d, leaving a cif fleiency of £485 2s 10d. The CAUBO of failure as alleged by the bankrupt, was " the loss of the action brought ty me against my landlord.' Mr Johnson, the assistant official receiver, intimated to the Registrar that he did not wish to question the debtor. Mr R P Cockerell instructed by Mr H L Riley, St Helens, who appeared for the landlord, Captain Michael Hughes, examined the debtor at great length, apparently with the object of ascertaining the debtor's exact financial position, both ,before and after the action. The debtor admitted tfhat the farm had been " so as yen could live on it," tut he denied having , made much out of it. Mr Johnson said it WRi only fair to the bankrupt to say that even after deducting from his assets a certain amount,for estimated loss or realisation, he had a sur- plus apart from the costs of the action, of £l3O. Further questioned by Mr Cockerel!, the d btor said he had not endeavoured to asctrtain, previous to bring- ling the action, what he would have to pay in case he lost. Counsel had advised that he had a good cause of laction. In reply to Mr Browne, who appeared for the debtor, Pownall said his credit was gomt all over the town, and apart from his liabilities in connectinn with the action he was more than solvent. The action was the cause of all the mischief. Mr Cockerel': You should not have brought it. Debtor : It was me losing it instead of them. The examination was declared closed.

Warrington. Licencing Transfers. At the City Borough Police Court on Monday the magistrates granted a transfer of the spirit licence from the representative of the late John Lees Hall formerly manager of the firm of Daniel Melia and Company, Horsemarket street to Mr ' Edward Melia, a director of the Company. Thomas W. Chantry, liorgemarket Street was granted the transfer of an off wine licence from the late Mr William Chantry. Oddfellowship Bro James Bramtiall, Grand master, presided at the quarterly meeting of the Warrington District of the National Independent Order ot oddfellows, which was held on Saturday at the Crown and Sceptre Hotel, and Bro Walter Duncan, D G M., occupied the vice-chair. The lodges were repre- sented thus: PPGME E Pester, P G, T Gregson, Bro Thomas Wright, G M, Bro W H Powney. N G, P 0, George Roberts, P G, Peter Kennedy, PPG M Thomas Knight, PO, Joseph Higham, and Bro William Pennington, GM. Among the visitors P G oteorge Hands, and P P 0 M, James Smith,treasurer of the district. After the accounts had been passed, a pleasing presentation took place, consisting of aPP 0 M's jewel, to the retiring Grand master, Bro El' win E Pester.—The Grand master before call- ing upon the secretary,Bro Thomas Dickinson CS to perform the ceremony remarked that no brother was more deserving of recognition.—l he Secretary said he was afraid he could not do justice to the recipient. Ever since they had become acquainted with each other, the ties of Oddfellowship had been more and more closely knitted. He trusted this small gift would induce him to endeavour to still further promote the interests of the society (Applause.) -13 TO Pester thanked his friends very heartily for their kind expressions, and hoped they would believe him when he said that he had mat with nothing but kindhess during the term of .office. lie bad striven to do his best for them, and although retiring from district office, he would still work on to help the society. It should be added that Bro Pester was elected to represent the district at the Annual Movable Committee, to be held at Huddersfield in Whit-week.

Lymm Urban District Oouncil Elections. Elections ot Urban District Councillors took place in two wards at Lynam on ftturday. Mr John R Smith and a band of helpers aided Mr Smith, whilst Mr Underwood had little support, The result showed that whereas he had previously beaten a very strong cand id at e, on Saturday he only received 26 votes against 190 given to his opponent. —ln the Lymm Ward the reault was a closer fight, the returning officer Mr DI ullard (clerk to the Urban District Council) declaring the result to be :—Rev G B Thur.ton, 107; Mr Gotobed, 79 —1 he usual votes of thanks to the returning officer were passed. There were no spoilt papers in either ward.

latttrs to tb bitor. -M. Henry Pontet Prccolomini Deceased.

Sir,—Many ef your readers who are lovers of music may not have heard of the sad death on the 9th tilt in impoverished circumstances of the gifted composer of " Ora Pro Nada," " Whisper and I shall hear," " Eternal Rest," " The toilers," " The last muster," and many other popular songs, who was buried on the 15th nit at Mortlake Cemetery. I should like on behalf of the widow and children of the late composer who are left totally unprovided for to appeal to such of the readers of your estimable paper who cognizsat of the ' composer's worth and the pleasure and benefit the musical world has derived from his compositions might desire to assist in raising the fund which has been opened on behalf of the widow and children by the London City and Midland Bank, Leonard-place, Kensington. If yeu will kindly find space in your valu- able paper to make this appeal I shall be much obliged.—l am yours faithfully, W. HUBERT SMITE. 10, Fenchurch Buildings, London, 8.0.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
The Education Bill.

Sir,—! am sorry that the Government have brought in an Education Bill which would make the councils of boroughs over ten thousand the education authorities. The councils have not been elected for that purpose aid I do uot think that it is desirable that such a great res- ponsibility should be placed upon them. I think that the education authority ought to be specially elected, and I believe that the ratepayers would elect men far better suited for the work. If such an authority should abuse the trust delegated to them the corporation and the ratepayers, could call them to account. But if such additional powers were given to the councils of boroughs it woula be almost impossible to apply a check in this fashion. This is indeed a most important subject, extending beyond the education of the country, and I trust that Parliament will not allow the Bill to pass unless the education authority be a specially elected independent authority. If you will allow me I will give some reasons why the education authority ought to be specially elected. We only allow a man one vote because two votes would be unfair to others, and the man with two votes would have more than his share of power. Yet an elected body often fails to express the real desires of the electorate, because many do not vote disinterestedly. Men who care more for position than for the public welfare, pander to selfishness and nrojodice and intimi- date, in order to obtain the positions which they covet, and under existing circumstances often obtain mars votes than better men, not because they are better liked or more fitted, but because better men would not, could not be so very mean. In consequence of the inordinate desire for elected public positions the ratepayers are improperly influenced, the country has tried to prevent bribery and corruption but it cannot make men give disinterested votes. It cannot prevent men who covet position appealing to the selfish interests of ratenayers and to prejudice. It cannot prevent such men sacrificing the weltare of the community for per- sonal otj, cte. But the powers which the ratepayers delegate ought to be divided.—Yours &c.. April Bth, 1902.

S. KENYON,

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington are in the final for the South• West Lanca- shire Cup once more—if there is to be any final—for Widnes have yet to make tw points in order to draw level. Though we have twice this season failed to defeat the Chemicals, I think Warrington will make no mistake about the third time if the cup and medals are the stake. Widnes are nothing like so strong a team as the Wirepullers, and with the latter as at present con- stituted they ought to have no difficulty in gaining the verdict. It was by no means an easy task to take four points out of Wigan in the course of last week, but the feat was accofhplished, the balance of points in War- rington's favour being 17-5. Last Saturday's match was on the Wigan ground, and though the weather con- ditions were miserable Warrington gave a smart exposi- tion of the game and won deservedly by 7 points to nil.

The rain militated considerably against the gate, and Springfield Park was only peopled by about 2,000 specta- tors instead of three times that number as expected. Warrington left nothing to chance and took their strongest possible team, Burgess coming in again at half-back, and Hockenhall partnering Fish on the left. The change worked well and the back play of the visitors was very effective throughout the afternoon. Wigan had a considerable advantage forward but were outclassed in the rear, and it was astonishing how narrowly the Wigan line escaped being crossed on numerous occasions.

A feature of the game was the fine full-back display of Hallam who fairly excelled himself under the worst , conditions possible. His fielding was most accurate, while his punts seldom failed to send the heavy ball half the length of the field. Burgess frequently broke away from the pack and gave an excellent display, though he was a trifle too vigorous in his methods on ,one or two occasions. Still his value was unmistakable, and with the men now at their command Warrington /should finish up creditably.

The South-West Lancashire League table up to-date fas follows : Points

P. W. L. D. Fo-r—A—g-st. pts Warrington lO ... 7 ... 2 ... 1 ... 74 ... 42 ... 15 Widnes Wigan 3 Runcorn 9 Leigh St. Helens

The fourth round for the Northern Union Cup did not furnish any surprises on Saturday. Broughton Rangers easily triumphed over Swinton who seemed to miss Valentine very much, while Salford did about the smartest performance of the day in defeating Huddersfield by j 3 tries to 2 tries at Fartown, the last points being obtained while D Smith was absent through injury. Batley comfortably accounted for Castieford, and once again figure in the semi-final. Are they destined to win the cup again I wonder ? The other tie was between Heckmondwike and Hunslet on the ground of the former, a draw of 2 points each being the result. In the replay on Wednesday, however, Hunslet advanced a step further without great dill- culty.

• The semi-Calais to-day are between Salford and Batley atlOldham, and Broughton Rangers 7 Hunslet at Wake- field. Both should furnish close and interesting con- tests. If the two Yorkshire clubs win,the final will be at: Headingley again, Salford or Rochdale being the venue should one or both Lancashire clubs survive.

Association Football.

The final tie in connection with the Warrington Cup Competition will be played on the Earlestown football ground this Saturday, the contesting clubs being Winnington Park (holders 1898-9), and St Helens Recs. (combination team). Kick off 3-30 p.m. Referee, Mr T P Forbes. The St Helens Rees Committee recognizing the strength of the Cheshire Club have decided to place a strong team in the field, with a view to winning the Cup, and as Winnington Park intend to make a special effort to again secure the trophy, a good game is antici- pated, and the match should be well worth a visit. The Cup and Medals will be presented on the ground immediately after the match.

WARRINGTON'S BUDGET. The Finances of the Borough. Interesting Statement by Alderman Shaw-Green.

No Increase in the Rates.

Tuesday's meeting of the Warrington Town Council was what has become known as" Budget Night." In presenting the estimates of the Finance and Estate Committee,Alderman bHAW-GREEN (the Chairman) made an exhaustive speech. I rise, he said, with some satisfaction, to propose the adop- tion of the estimates for the coming year. It has been a difficult task to get all ready in the time at ' our disposal, for the last financial year did not close until Monday last, and until the balances were all made up we could not tell what ought to be done. One of our local papers, some days ago, announcing the postponement of the Council meeting, said that the meeting to-night would be an important one, as the Budget weuld be introduced, and the rates, for the coming year fixed. That, surely, is not all that makes it important. For first we approve of our committees doing the work suggested in the estimates, and then we levy the rate to pay for the same. To-night, amongst our other duties, we take financial stock. We recall the past, and try to anticipate the future. May I suggest we should do so to see wherein we may improve it. It is the only opportunity afforaed us for fair con- sideration of what the Council does as a whole, for, split up as we are in our several committees, we do not realize the magnitude of our operations, nor the cost— the work entails. Comparing the actual expenditure with last year's estimates it is only necessary to refer to two of the committees who have overspent, and that in no hostile was'. COMMITTEES WHO HAVE OVERSPENT THEIR

E TI MATES.

The Cemetery Committee has spent over £5OO more than it estimated, and then we have the Town Hall Committee,l was going to say as is usual overspending £5OO also; but there are special rea- sons for both these. The Cemetery Committee has paid the compensation for burial fees to the sextons, who havebeen retired,and woichwill not:occur again These fees will in the future add to our revenue; and the Town Hall Committee has painted and decorated this building, and completed the electric light installation, &c. Our revenue, too, has been well maintained. Notwithstanding the constant and regular growth year after year we have again to report an increase. The market tolls, &c, have I given us £173 extra; the weights and measures fees, £34; the baths, £43; the cemetery, £137. At the same time, also, the important discussion which took place upon the presentation of the budget 12 months ago, and the determination then expressed by the various members of the Council, that the committees should exercise vigilance and care, has, I believe, been faithfully carried out ; consequently our expendi.ure_. has not ex— ceeded the limits which we then laid down. The Council will also remember that at that time we looked all our indebtedness fully in the face. including the heavy liability for interest on the property purchasing account, the second instal- ment of which appears this year. INTERCHANGE OF IDEAS AMONGSI • OFFICIALS

In connection with the future expenditure, I, for one, anticipate considerable benefit to arise from the resolution, which, I trust, will be honour- ably carried out, that the responsible heads of the departments shall regularly meet for the inter- change of ideas upon the work which the Council undertakes. (Hear, hear.) I would not forget specially in this the idea that it will prevent tte growth of a parochial disposition, with respect to each committee ; that no committee will con- clude that it has a special and separate existence, having the right to do the best for itself, but rather that each will remember they are only parts of one great whole. (Hear, hear.) The object of each being the benefit of the community, none more powerful than, but each needful to the rest.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE AMOUNT OWING TO THE BANK.

Last year it was announced that we owed the bank over £130,000, borrowed temporarily to pay our expenditure upon capital charges, until it was advantageous to take up the money upon mortgage. That money was, theretore, simply owing to the bank, and not to mortgages, and it made no differ- ence to the Corporation, except a slight difference in the interest, but as our terms with the bank had been arranged on literal lines, it was profitable to adopt this course. During the year we have spent no less than £133,000 of capital largely upon what we trust will be revenue producing work, and I have no doubt it will occasion some surprise to the members of the Council to be told that litttle less than a quarter of a million of money has been taken up upon. mortgagees during the vest just closed.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
THE IgTIMATES 'CRU EINISEb.

And now let us look at the estimatee for the coming year. On the borough rate account, the estimate shows a net increase of about £6B, but the estimated receipt., and the cast, in the bank reduce the demand to £1,660 less than last year, Last year, as you will see, we owed the bank £1,030, whilst this year wa have in the bank £1,195, and this has made all the difference. Our de- rnand upon the overseers is for £20,605 16s 10d. The Council will probably rememb. r that the overseers dipped very teavily into their reserve balance last year, and they consequently feel compelled to levy a rate at the same amount as last year. With the exception of the Watch Committee, there is generally speaking, an all-round reduction. The increase in this committee's estimate is chiefly provision for the extra cost of main- taining the new police premises. These items are esti- mates only, but we hope the amount will be sufficient.

THE COST OF THE POLICE BUILDINGS.

We constantly hear comments about the new con- stabulary buildings and the style in which the work is being carried out. it is undoubtedly a beautiful building and one of which any borough may be r mud. Also it is quite true that such buildings ought to be on a liberal mile, but there are limits, and whilst I would not say we have passed proper bounds, we have, at all events, very considerably passed the estimate. We had the rum of £25,000 given us as the total cost to complete it, and many of us thought that too much to put in hand at once,but on having the figures taken put recently we are informed that we have actually a known ex- penditure of £29,200. How much will have to be added to that time only will tell. THE CONTRIBUTION FROM: THE GAS DEPARTMENT.

Turning now to the Improvement Rate account, it will be seen that we have a slightly larger estimate than last sear, but the Council will remember that last year we were in debt to the bank, which, fortunately, has been cleared off, and now, though it is a most in- significant tiumme have a credit balance. Still it makes a wonderlal difference in the net result. The net sum is £30,775, but in order to get it dowa we have had to string up the receipts to what raay be described as their full limit, and have• inserted as to come from the Gag Department £2,000- The CCUPCii will no douht renember the animated die cuPsion that took place 12 months' ago as to tt e amount the Gas Department should provide. INCRE&SED cosr OF SANITARY WORK.

Let me now show, by way of comparison, how the toot of the Health and Sanitary Departments gradually I grows with our population. We strive our utmost, to improve the surreal:Kline of our people, we seek to secure. them from disease by purifying their homes, steaming their yard spaces, and removing everything injurious, and our expenditure en these and other sanitary work has grown in ten years from £11,296 to £17,771. The Markets Committee is proposing to pro- vide further important pub:ic sanitary accommodation, and the sum of £450 is put in their estimate for this purpose. Unlike all other cornin4tees, it is not able to get the sanction of tt• Local Governmext Beard for all its capitsi expenditure, and by means of a sinking fund repay the money over a tolerably long period. It is held that any expenditure on this heod by the Markets Committee must be paid for out of it revenue. Some time ago we sanctioned the paving of the old fair ground, and last year the roofing in of the north end af the market, and at this moment there is unpaid on these accounts about £2,400. We are pay- ing it off at £6OO a year, but it tells heavily against the benefit we ought to have from the market tolls, for £6OO represents nearly id in the £ ; and to this £450 and It means lid in the £ for this year. It had also been sug- gested that the Highway Department were likely to • benefit by the improvement of the thoroughfares from the recent paving by the Tramways Committee, and that a reduction might be expected, but the committee has felt constrained to submit the same figures as last year for macadam, -sett paving and c >nage. Notwith- standing the excess of the estimate over last year upon the improvement rateaccount we see no reason to fear any failure in the various sources of revenue, and con- sequently, we have decided to ask the Council to meet the expenditure asked for with a rate at the same amount as last year. THE SEWERS RATE.

The last item to claim our attention is the general sewers rate. This rate, as has often been explained, is levied generally at 2d in the £ one year, and 4d the next; but having a fair balance in the bank, we have again decided to ask the Couaeil to use up that balance, and levy a rate at. 2d only. The effect of this is that the combined rate will be at the same amount as last year, and the satisfaction I referred to in clueing arises from the fact that in spite of many diffi- culties we are enabled to do this. In referring to the revenue producing departments it is only fair to mention that, in consequence of one of those strange and unac- countable freaks, the House of Commons, whilst giving us the longest term for the repayment of our sinking fund ia 199, did not grant us the usual privilege of suspension of the sinking fund upon the water under- tAing. These are usually granted for five years, conse- quently we have to provide out of the revenue of the water department at once the re-payment of the sink- ing fund as well as the interest. This has made a con- , siderable difference, and certainly has affected oar ' calculations. We fully relied upon the gradual growth I of the prsfit during the suspension, and the unexpected ' and unusual charge naturally made us feel slightly salamis.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
WILL THE TRAMS PAY ?

One of the features of the cew financial year will be that of locomotion. The electrical tramcars will be run- log very soon, and our citizens will then have an easy and cheap mans ef getting out to the boundaries of the town, and thug to the fresh air of the country. Those also who have business will find it more convenient to get quickly to and fro, and whilst many people profess to consider the working of them will entail a call upon the rates,there are amongst us those who have carefully considered the new departure, and who hold strongly the epinion that after a very short time they will become a source of profit. This opinion is, I think, borne out by the fact that applications for the privilege of norking the traras have been received, the would-be lessees offering the Corporation fair terms for the con- cession. Surely if it would be profitable to a non-resid- ent company it must be more so to one of our own committees ; but whether profitable or not it does not effect the present estimates, for if a profit result it will coma to Ilse good next year and if a lose we shall have to face it then. Then also we have not included in our estimates anything for elementary education. What may be in store for us in this matter is more than any- one can tell, but it needs no prophetical vision to say that we shall soon have to face a fresh impost on that account.

THE CORPORATION EDUCATION BILL.

is already well known our estimates prove fo the cost of promoting a 13111 to enable the Corporation t make prOvision for the districts at present insuffleientlY provided with schools. This, we thought, would be a small matter and our Bill was to be of a temporary character, and merged into the Government measure when that became law. The very important matter of the adjustment of the financial relations on the sever- ance of the Borough from the county has occupied us considerably during the past year. COMPENSATION TO THE COUNTY AUTHORITIES. It may not be generally known that on taking up our position as a county borough we had to give compensa- tion to the county for the loss of revenue from us, and for the share of the debts, Acc., %Mich the county including ourselves are liable for. The eraparation of the accounts necessary for this adjustment has been carefully, and think I may say skilfully, worked out by the Borough Treasurer with the loyal assistance of our able Town Clerk, and many interviews have taken place to settle the differences inseparable from an important and intricate matter of this kind. I am pleased to say that we have now reached a stage when it may be said a settlement is in sight, but when I mention that the capital sum involved is according to our view nearly £24 000, the Council will see that it is no trifling matter. Tne Corporation did not enter upon the widening of the streets, the water extensions, the electrical lighting, the tranaways, &c., without first sitting down to count the cost, and seeing that if were able to meet them. We have often said we did not face the future with misgiving, provided we had full confidence in each other, and that 1 determination to promote the general welfare of the COM- munity I have previously referred to. The burden and the responsibility of our civic life is constantly increas- ing. And this means increased care and personal effort for each.

A NEWSPAPER'S SCARE.

It is very easy to sit end criticize the government in which men take no share, but those whe devote their leisure to the well-being of the community,and who face duties bringing to them anxiety and care, at least ("s- -tarve to be respected for their works' sake and for the sacrifices they make. Whether or no, there are same pleasant reflections, and though the labour in conduct- ing our many organiz itiOdS is great,yet having achiecea that which we sought brings a certain and sure reward. In times of stress and difficulty every rift in the clouds, though unimprrtant under ordinary conditions, is a beam of joy, and on the other hand, every additional trouble, though small and without effect generally, becomes a burden, so when another of our lobal papers, entirely without reason,and certainly without the slight- est poseible chance of forecasting the financial conditions, in any way announced that there was the probability of an increase in the rates of 9.1 in the £ a scare and sen- sation was aroused that brought an unusual and unnee- cessary amount of correspondence to at least one member of the Council. However wise and farseeing I generally, this shows the imperfection of all things human. During the, past few weeks in - common with many other members of the Council, I have been frequently questioned by anxious rate- payers as to the prospects, and they would scarcely credit me when I said it was absolutely irappseible to say. This anxiety was not to be wondered at, for the increase of last year brought up the rate to a 'oigh fi,,,ure, and the addition to the income tax following immediately thereupon made people who did not other- wise generally cancern themselves wear an appearance of anxiety. WARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL. The Corporation and " Fair " Contracts. #

The moathly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held in the Druids Hall, Sankey- street, on Tuesday evening, Dar J Edwardson presiding over a good attendance.

VIE CORPORATION AND TRAM& UNION OFFICIALS.

The SECRETARY reported that the Exc,cutive Commit- tee were taking steps to have the following resolution brought before the Town Council :—" That it be an instruction to all committees of this Council, in the event of any dispute with the employee of the Corpora- tion, that the official representatives of the men shall conduct all interviews, and arrange term en their behalf." The CIAIRMAN said the members of the Committee were satisfied that the time had arrived when both the Town Council and Board of Guardians ought to accept any representatives whom trade uniosists wished to officially represent them. Tee committee had eucceeded in obtaining a pledge from a member c f the Town Coun- cil, that he would give notice to move the resolution at the May meeting of the Council. The same gentleman was trying to get a member of the Council te second the resolution, and ethers to support it. The committee hoped the resolution would be carried and that in future they would not have grievances put off for twelve Months like the one at Longford had been. If the resolution were passed such actions as that of the Sani- tary Committee at the present time would be avoided. WARRINGTON'S UNEMPLOYED.

I Toe following letter was read from the Town Clerk (Mr J Lyon Whittle) with reference to the unemployed of the town :—"Reterring to your letter of the act' ult. I have to inform you that I laid. the same before the several committees of the Council who have the employ- meat of workmen and that with the exception of the Paving and Sewerage Committees nous of the committees are able to provide employment for any additional workmen. With respect to the Paving and Sewerage Committee I am instructed to say that they can nod employment for a number of paviors on application to the Borough Surveyor. I have made inquiries and have ascertained that trade had improved within the las; fourteen days and that in the opinion of those competent to form an , opinion there is likely to be a still further improvement. ' I hope this will be the case." Mr JO SUTTON, clerk to the Board of Guardians, wrote :—" As promised, I laid your letter of 14',Ibruary 27th before the Guardians at their Board meeting on March 26th and was directed to reply that the Gear- diens can only treat each application to them for relief , upon its merits. Their powers in such matters are limited by statute."

THE C )RONATION FESTIVITIES. C)FINCIL TO TAKE NO PART.

Delegates fro n the eugineers.bakers, gas workers and general labourers, wheelwrights and smiths, wire- drawers, railway servants, and jain,rs societies, reported that the members of their branches bed decided to take no pert as Trade Union- ists in the proposed procession on Coronation Day. The reason generally given was that many members of the Societies wee also members of Friendly Societies and would prefer to take part in the procession as mem- bers of the latter organize:ions. The Cousmil decided to take no part as a body in the procession, and the Secretary was instructed to write to the Mayor informing him that the decision to take no part had been arrived at by the Societies affiliated with the Council.

TLIE CORPORATION AND "FAIR " CONTRACT.

ALLEGED BREACH OF CONDITIONS.

A long discesiion teak place with reference to the " fair wages " clause which is usually inserted in Cor- poration contracts. The SECRETAUY said he had written to the Town Clerk with reference o the matter,:and in reply he was informed that the following clause was in operation with reference to the contracts : (12). The contractors shall pay all persons engaged or employed , by:them on the works the rates of wages recognized in the district, and shall observe each hourseof labour as are generally accepted as fair in each trade, and shall from time to time, whenever required so to do by the Council, produce to them such evidence as they deem sufficient that they are paying such wages and observ- ing such hours. In reply to a further communication the Tower CLERIC said the above condition was not inserted in all con- tracts. The condition referred to " the standard rate of wages and such hours of labour as are generally accepted as fair in the district of Warringten in the respective trades engaged in carrying out the works." Be did not know whether these differed from those recognized as the f. trade union rate." One firm had received the contract for supp'ying, police clothing and the other for printing. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A Society.

There was an average attendance at the meeting of this Society on Sunday afternoon. The ehair was taken by Mr David Owen, who referred to the feet that while the greatest rulers bad established their greatness by force, Jesus Christ who had exercised the greatest toff u- eons on the lives of men, was able to de it by love. The I Rev J F Matthews was the speaker for the occasion, and at the outset expressed his sympathy with the mem- bers at the absence of the President (Rev John Yonge). Ile proceeded to refer to the Education Bill, and his reirtarks thereon will be found in another column.—The service was also taken part in by Mr B Farrar and Mr 9 Rowlands. Mr Rfi Pearson presided at the organ. Camden Sunday School, Runcorn— Anniversary Services.

In connection with the above schools the annul sere roans were preached on Sunday by the Rey H Gulley, London, general secretary to the Sunday School Union. Fortunately for the success of the "sermons " the weather was beautifully fine and at all the services there were exceedingly large congregations. The children had for soree time been carefully prepared to play their part end the reselt was a first class musical treat in the way of choral singing.—ln the morning the reverend gentleman delivered an instructive discourse on the text, "The words I say unto you they are spirit and they are lift." The sninister briefly showed that while men strove after education, amusement and such like things there still remained a deeper craving in men's hearts which could only be satisfied by the spirit of Christ.—ln the evening a Most interest- ing discourse was delivered from the 144 Psalm, verse 12, "That our eons may be as plants grown up in their youth, that our daughters may be as corner stones polished after the similitude of a palace." :The preacher said it was impossible to exaggerate the importance of his theme. The moral and spiritual life of the youth of the nation was of importance to the boosts in which they lived, the Churches to which they were joined, end the nation of which they formed a part. Ile wished to speak that night to those who were no longer little chil— dren, but to those who had come to the parting of the ways and knew it. Ile wished to pint out to such God's will concerning them. The text said "that our sons may be as pleats" lot weeds or thorns. Some of the most poisonous weeds were found in the most sheltered homes, and very often the hemlock and the rue were found growing together in the same garden. Each had the power of extracting from the soil and the atmosphere that which in the one became deadly poison and the other a thing of beauty and grace. Similarly the self determining power of the Christian lay in him- self. While he was speaking his hearer could determine whether be would be a lower or a poisonous weed. Nothing blighted the youthful Christian like transgres- sion and nothing stunted the growth of character like vice. God pardened the sin but the results of trans• grenion were borne through life. Many a man sitting quietly in a pew bore in his nervous system and brain the ' result of early transgression. Regeneration never took out a single scar. And there would be twists and cracks in the character because) of the folly of early life. But even a twisted and gnarled piece of oak could be beauti- fully polished. There were far more ninin twisted morally threngh going wrong in early life than one im- agined. To grew up straight as a palm with nothing between one and God was a beautiful picture and could only; be accomplished by allaying a good root. Those who talked at the Urban Counoil.elearion that they did , not care what anybody said were the num who cared most, Those who were not concerned with the impres- sion they were making ought not to live. What was infinitely more important than making an impression was the cause or root of it all, which grew unseen. Many men were all top and no root• Young men sent to Liverpool, London or Birmingham, without any deep moral root were sure to go down. If the conditions of the plant were right fruit would appear at the right time and place in its own way. There would he the fruit of Christian he'll and love. It was men of deep root that were wanted to-day in the churches, in cersmerce, and at St Stephen's. Their daughters were the corner stones and the corner stone was the one above all else in the build- 'no. It had the m-et conspicuous place, was the most carefully cut and the most beautifully carved. Where- ever the Christian religion had gone they would find that woman bad been socially elevated as the companion and not theislave orrnen. Christianity invested womanhood and childhood with at3ignity and honour they had never possessed previous to the incarnati en. The world had not been moved by strength alone but by intellectual strength and tenderness. The greatest statemen corn- 'Lined intellectual strength with tenderness, and the woman could thus do service which the church parson could never do. Tl•ere was a place for each womanly heart in the'great temple of their God. If their sons were the granite their daughters were the cement. If their eons were the graceful columns supporting the building their daughters were the uniting corner stones. The great gusrdiens of the purity of their homes were the mothers, sisters and daughters. The workers in the churches should go on with their Plant- ing. They should go to the quarry and bring neglected stones—neglected children—to Christ. One of the glorious features of Methodism was that they had never gone across the road and pulled out tire stones from their neighbour's church and put them;in their own and called it church building. They had always gone to the quarry. He exhorted his hearers to commence the Christian race even if on no higher ground than that of self-interest. A service wee also held on Monday night which was largely attended. The school maintained its deservedly high reputation for harmony, balance and time. The pieces had been carefully selected and were pretty and refined. The choir wee most successful in the anthems "With heart and voice," "Wake the son," "Calling softly calling," and " Frost ye in the Lord." Too much praise cannot be given for the manner in which the various sections of the choir had been trained. It is interesting to know that the membership of the Sunday school numbers 500 scholars. There are 42 teachers and the expenses for the year total £52 is 4d: The collections for the day amounted to £45 12s 63, being a satisfactory increase on last year.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A special meeting of the Urban District Council was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown en Wednes- day. Mr W Collingwood presided and there were present Dr Latham, Messrs T Ormand,N biddeley,W Gilmour, J Simpson. W Thompson, E T Evans and C Cole (clerk).

===== 0002601_19020411_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GARSWOOD COLLIERY DISASTER.

Before commencing business the Chairman said that since the last meeting a terrible disaster had happened in the immediate neighbourhood. He re- ferred to the Garswood Hall Colliery accident. He thought they would not be expressing the feeling of the township unless they moved a vote of con- dolence and sympathy with those who were suffer- ing from the effects of he accident. (Hear, hear.) They could sy mpathise very deeply, probably more than any other place in the country as they were so intimate with the industry, and depended so much upon the men who had been such terrible sufferers in this catastrophe. fie therefore asked them to agree to a vote of condolence and sym- pathy with the widows and children. The vote was agreed to in silence, GAS COMMITTEE.

Mr Thompson moved the confirmstion of the minutes of the Gas Committee held that day. The following tenders were accepted. Messrs Clayton, sons and Co., Limited, Hunslet, Leeds, two additional lifts to as Holders. Messrs. Briggs, Limited, Halifax two settings of retorts. Mr U li'airclough, Warrington, for extension of Re- tort House. It was resolved to recommend to the Council that they apply to the Local Government Board for tto sanction of a loan of £I,OOO to carry out these special works, and also for the sum of £5OO for the supply of gas stoves to the public. Mr Siddeley seconded and t6e minutes Were ap- proved.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
HORSE AMBULANCE.

The Clerk brought before the Council the corres- pondence from Superintendent Cocker with regard to the supply of a Horse Ambulance, and after some discussion it was decided to refer the matter to the General Purposes Committee to•consider the details. It app4ared from the correspondencl that the police intended to provide the Ambulance and present it to the Council, the latter to provide the horse and the housing accommodation.

===== 0002601_19020411_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CORONATION.

Mr Gilmour asked what it was intended to do with reward to the Coronation celebration.

The Chairman remarked that a meeting of the committee would be held shortly to discuss the matter.

THE EDUCATION ACT.

Mr Ormand proposed and Mr J Simpson seconded that the Council petition the House of Commons praying for an amendment of section 1 of the Edu- cation Bill (England and Wales 1902), which stated that Non-County Boroughs with a population of 10,000 and Urban District Councils with a popula- tion of 20,000 be education authorities, the dedred amendment to place Urban Dikrrict Councils on the same footing as Non-County Boroughs The motion was carried and will be sent to Colonel Pilkingtou, M.P.

THE EARLESTOwN STABBING CASE TUE CRARGE REDUCED TO ASSI.UL T. FINE INFLICTED.

Barrett again appeared before the magistrates at Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday. Several witnesses were called to give evidence, Constable Clarke stating that when he arrested Barrett be said " I have done the same in South Africa, aud I'll do it again in self- defenee." For the defence, Mr Longton sud it was a disgraceful case, and was caused by drink. After ttds men had been drinking heavily they quarrelled, and de- fendant alleged that Rush received his cuts by the broken glasses and bottles during the melee that fol- lowed.—The bench said it was a very serious offence, and prisoner might have been standing there for murder instead of assault (the charge baying been reduced by consent of the police to one of assault). In considera- tion of his previous good character he would taa fined 40s and costs, and he (the chairman) :trusted it would stay him against that cursed drink,which seemed to be at the bottom of all the injury and mischief.

NOTES BY THE NAY AT

EARLESTOwN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

- Time is going on and we in this neighbourhood are still without any information as to what is pro- posed in connection with the Coronation celebra- tion Mr Gilmour raised the question at a special meeting of the Council, on Wednesday afternoon, but the only satisfaction no got was that the com- mittee wh3 ha,l the matter in hand would shortly hold a meeting to discuss it. Tne committee will not have to lose any time if they intend to organie a celebration worthy of the occasion and of the town.

At last the Council have decided to make a move in the direction of supplying gas stoves to the pub- lic, Of aourse, this does not mean to say they will get them without payment. They will have to buy them at the market price, but they will be allowed time in which to complete the purchase. The method will be on the hire purchase system, and it a person obtains a stove, say costing £B, he can-take say five years in which to pay this amount to the Council. If, before the five years have expired, or the pur- chase is completed, the person having possession of the stove, desires to discontinue its use, the money paid will be considered as hire, and there will be no refund. No doubt this will be an advantage to the public, but it would be a greater advant- age it the Council supplied the stoves free, as they do in other places, and what other Councils do, surely they can copy at Newton.
